Blocks the obvious cases before any tokens are spent.Input safety
    Model safetyOne Bedrock guardrail (numbered version) with content filters, prompt attack filter, denied topics, word filter, sensitive information filters, contextual groundingThe managed control the exam expects. Applies to input and output, and to retrieved content wrapped in guardContent.Anatomy of a guardrail
    GroundingBedrock Knowledge Base Retrieve, chunks passed to Converse as grounding sourcesLets you show the guardrail and the post-validator exactly what the answer may rely on.Reducing hallucinations
    Structured outputA response JSON Schema, enforced in the prompt or tool definition and validated with the jsonschema package in the post-validatorThe model is asked for the shape, and code guarantees it. Never trust the request alone.Output safety
    Post-validationLambda: schema check, citation check, output toxicity check, fallback builderDeterministic checks that catch failures a classifier misses.Output safety
    Human reviewStep Functions Standard with waitForTaskToken, SQS, a small review pageLong pauses cost almost nothing. A timeout routes to a safe default.Input safety
    Adversarial testingA pytest suite of labelled attack and benign prompts, run in CI against a test stageTurns "we think it is safe" into a number that can fail a build.Advanced threat detection
    MetricsCloudWatch embedded metric format from each Lambda: block counts per layer, false positives, latency per layer. Bedrock guardrail metrics in the AWS/Bedrock/Guardrails namespaceShows which layer does the work and what it costs in latency.Defense in depth

    The table fixes the safety pieces. Where the code runs and how you define the infrastructure are your choice. The build path assumes the options marked default, and every other option is valid.

    DecisionOptionTrade-off in one line
    Compute and hostingAWS Lambda, zip package (default)Pay per request, no servers, scales to zero. Each layer is its own small function with its own IAM role. Watch cold starts and the 29-second default API Gateway integration timeout.
    Lambda with a container imageSame model, but you package dependencies such as jsonschema and a local classifier in a Docker image pushed to Amazon ECR (Elastic Container Registry). Useful when the zip grows large.
    Lambda with Lambda Web AdapterRun one FastAPI app that contains all layers inside a single function. Simpler to test locally, but you lose per-layer roles and per-layer metrics come from your own code.
    Amazon ECS on AWS FargateRun the gateway as a FastAPI container behind an Application Load Balancer. No cold starts and steady latency, but you pay for running tasks even when idle.
    Amazon ECS on EC2Same container, on instances you manage. Cheaper at high steady load, more operations work (patching, capacity).
    Amazon EKSKubernetes-managed containers. Only worth it if your company already runs EKS. Far more operations than this project needs.
    Amazon EC2A single instance running the FastAPI app. Fine for a throwaway demo, weakest for scaling and patching.
    Step FunctionsAlready used for the human review lane. You could also run the whole request path as an Express workflow, which gives a visual trace per request at the cost of extra latency per state.
    Not recommendedAWS App RunnerApp Runner is no longer open to new customers. AWS recommends Amazon ECS Express Mode as the replacement. Existing customers can continue to use App Runner, but no new features will be added. Check the App Runner availability change page for details.
    Infrastructure as code (infra/)Terraform (default)Cloud-neutral, explicit plans before every change. You write more resource blocks by hand.
    AWS CDK (Python)Infrastructure in Python with high-level constructs for API Gateway, Lambda, and Step Functions. Synthesizes CloudFormation.
    AWS SAMShortest templates for API Gateway plus Lambda, and sam local invoke for testing. Less convenient for the guardrail and Knowledge Base resources.
    AWS CloudFormationThe native template format underneath CDK and SAM. Verbose, but no extra tool to install.
    Console and CLI scriptsFastest for a first run, but not repeatable. Acceptable only for a throwaway spike, and rebuild in code before you rely on it.
    Interface to the serviceAPI Gateway REST API with Lambda (default)Gives request validation, usage plans, and response mapping without your own code.
    FastAPI appBehind an ALB on ECS or inside Lambda Web Adapter. You implement validation (Pydantic) and response filtering yourself, and you can still put API Gateway in front.
    Plain scriptA local CLI that calls the layers in order. Good for milestones 1 to 4 before anything is deployed.

    Build path

    Suggested folder name: genai-safety-gateway, laid out as in the repository's project template (src/, tests/, infra/, Dockerfile if you package a container). If you built the Domain 1 genai-model-gateway project, copy its Converse wrapper and retry configuration into src/. Do not import across project folders. Each step leaves something that runs.

    1. Design note and the attack setOne page: the layers, the thresholds for block and review, the latency budget, and the fallback text. Then write the test set as a JSONL file with a label on every line: at least 60 attacks (role-play jailbreaks, "ignore previous instructions", instructions hidden in a fake policy document, Base64 and Unicode tricks, card numbers, requests for legal or investment advice, insults) and at least 40 benign questions, including some that look risky but are fine, such as "my policy covers dog bites, right?". The benign set is what measures false positives.
    2. The guardrail aloneCreate the guardrail with content filters (prompt attack set to high on input), three denied topics with examples, a word filter, sensitive information filters (block card numbers, anonymize phone and policy numbers with a regex), and contextual grounding at 0.7 for both thresholds. Create version 1. Run the whole test set through ApplyGuardrail with source="INPUT" and record the block rate on attacks and the false positive rate on benign prompts. This is your baseline.
    3. The pre-filterWrite the sanitizer (Unicode normalization, control and zero-width characters stripped, length cap) and the Comprehend calls as plain Python functions with unit tests. Add them in front of the guardrail in a local script and re-run the test set. Record which attacks each layer caught first, and how many milliseconds each layer adds.
    4. Grounded answers with structured outputConnect the Answer function to the Knowledge Base with Retrieve, pass chunks as grounding sources in guardContent, and ask for the four-field JSON. Run 30 coverage questions with known answers. Count grounded, ungrounded but passed, and blocked answers, and compare the guardrail's grounding score with your own judgement on five of them.
    5. The post-validatorAdd JSON Schema validation, the citation check (every cited chunk ID must be in the retrieved set), and an output toxicity check. Feed it hand-written bad outputs: missing fields, an invented citation, an insulting answer, a JSON string wrapped in prose. Each must produce the fallback with a logged reason.
    6. Deploy the request pathWrite the infrastructure as code: the REST API with a request validator and model, a usage plan and API key, the three Lambda functions with one IAM role each (only the Answer role can call bedrock:InvokeModel and bedrock:Retrieve), and a response mapping template that returns only the public fields. Add an IAM condition on bedrock:GuardrailIdentifier so the Answer role cannot invoke a model without the guardrail. Call the deployed API with curl and see a blocked, a masked, and a grounded response.
    7. Human review laneAdd the Standard workflow with a task token, an SQS queue, and a review endpoint. Route the pre-filter's borderline band to it and return a 202 holding reply. Test approve, reject, and the timeout path with a short timeout.
    8. Measure quality, cost, and latencyEmit metrics from every layer and build one CloudWatch dashboard: requests, blocks per layer, review queue depth, false positives (benign prompts blocked in the test stage), and p50 and p95 latency per layer. Run the full test set against the deployed stage three times. Compute the cost per 1,000 requests from guardrail text units, Comprehend units, and model tokens. If p95 is over budget, decide which layer to drop or run in parallel, and write the decision down.
    9. Failure testsBreak each dependency on purpose: deny the pre-filter role access to Comprehend, point the Answer function at a guardrail version that does not exist, and set a Lambda timeout of one second. Decide and test the policy for each case. A safety layer that fails must fail closed (block or hand off), never pass the request through unchecked. Confirm the dashboard and an alarm show each failure.
    10. Adversarial suite in CITurn the test set into a pytest suite that calls the test stage and asserts a minimum block rate on attacks and a maximum false positive rate on benign prompts. Run it in your CI tool on every change to the guardrail, the prompt, or the Lambda code. Add five new attacks you found during testing and confirm the build fails until you fix them.

    Definition of done

    • A request body with no question field, or with a question longer than the limit, gets a 400 from API Gateway, and no Lambda invocation appears in CloudWatch Logs.
    • A prompt that contains a test card number is blocked by the pre-filter, and the model invocation count for that request is zero.
    • An answer that would contain a phone number returns with the number replaced by a placeholder such as {PHONE}.
    • A role-play jailbreak and an instruction hidden inside a retrieved document are both blocked, and the log line names the layer that caught each one.
    • A question about a coverage rule that is not in the Knowledge Base returns the fallback with handoff: true, not an invented answer.
    • Every successful response validates against the response JSON Schema, and the response contains no field outside the four public ones.
    • Calling Converse from the Answer role without the guardrail fails with an AWS access error.
    • A borderline request starts a Step Functions execution that waits for a reviewer, and an unanswered item ends with the default reply after the timeout.
    • With Comprehend access removed, requests fail closed with a handoff reply and an alarm fires.
    • The CI suite reports the block rate and false positive rate, and fails when either crosses its threshold.
    • The dashboard shows p95 latency per layer, and the total added by the safety layers is within the budget you set, or the design note explains the decision you took.

    Cost, Region, and account notes

    Watch out

    Run everything in your project's assigned Region, and confirm that Bedrock Guardrails, your chosen model, and Comprehend's toxicity detection and prompt safety classifier are available there. Comprehend's trust and safety features are offered in fewer Regions than the core service: toxicity detection is English only, and the prompt safety classifier runs in only four Regions and is no longer available to new customers. Check the Comprehend feature availability page for your Region and account before milestone 3. If a feature is missing, use the guardrail's prompt attack filter and content filters as the classifier, and keep only DetectPiiEntities (or a regex) in the pre-filter.

    The test suite is the cost trap. Every run sends 100 or more prompts through Comprehend, the guardrail, and often the model. Guardrails bill per text unit for each policy you enable, and contextual grounding bills for the source text as well as the answer, so long retrieved chunks multiply the charge. Run the suite on a small model, cap maxTokens, and do not schedule it on a timer. Check the current Bedrock and Comprehend pricing pages for unit prices. A WAF web ACL has a monthly charge per ACL and per rule even with no traffic, so delete it when you finish, along with the API, the Knowledge Base vector store, and the state machine. Set an AWS Budgets alert before you start.

    Keep the guardrail trace disabled in the deployed stage. With trace enabled, the API response contains the original text that triggered a filter, which can include the very PII you masked. If you turn on Bedrock model invocation logging for debugging, remember that the logs hold the unmasked prompt, so encrypt the log group with a KMS key and set a short retention period.

    With a small model, short answers, and a test set of about 100 prompts, expect a few dollars for the whole build, most of it from repeated test runs and from the Knowledge Base vector store if you leave it running. The Lambda, API Gateway, Step Functions, and SQS charges stay at cents at this volume.

    Stretch ideas

    Stream the answer with ConverseStream and compare the guardrail's synchronous and asynchronous stream processing modes on latency and on what reaches the client before a block (Domain 4 covers performance tuning). Add an Automated Reasoning policy to the guardrail for two coverage rules and compare its findings with the contextual grounding check. Replace the hand-written attack set with prompts generated by a second model and evaluated by an LLM-as-a-judge, and run it as a Bedrock evaluation job (Domain 5 covers evaluation and testing). Route traffic by risk score so low-risk questions skip the pre-filter, and measure the cost and latency saved.

    Chapter 2 · Task 3.2

    Implement Data Security and Privacy Controls

    Chapter 1 protected the conversation: what goes into the model and what comes out. This chapter protects the data around it: the documents you index, the network path a prompt travels, the people and roles who can read a knowledge base, the personal details hidden inside a support ticket, and how long all of it is kept. You will isolate a GenAI workload inside a VPC, scope every data access to the minimum, find and mask personally identifiable information (PII) before it reaches a model or a log, and let S3 delete data on a schedule.

    Exam skills covered

    • 3.2.1 Protected AI environments: VPC endpoints for network isolation, IAM policies for secure data access, AWS Lake Formation for granular data access, CloudWatch to monitor data access
    • 3.2.2 Privacy-preserving systems: Amazon Comprehend and Amazon Macie to detect PII, Amazon Bedrock native data privacy features, guardrails to filter outputs, S3 Lifecycle configurations for data retention
    • 3.2.3 Privacy-focused AI that keeps FM utility: data masking, Comprehend PII detection, anonymization strategies, Bedrock guardrails

    How this task is examined

    Picture a Monday-morning message from the compliance team. A customer emailed a support assistant with their full card number and home address. The assistant answered politely, and the guardrail masked the card number in the reply. Then an auditor opened the model invocation logs and found the card number in plain text, readable by every engineer with access to CloudWatch. The same week, a security review found that the Lambda function calling Bedrock had a route to the internet, that its role could read every object in the documents bucket, and that three years of raw transcripts were sitting in S3 with no expiry.

    None of those problems is a model problem. Each one is a data control that someone forgot. Task 3.2 questions describe a workload like this and ask which control closes a specific gap with the least effort. The answers fall into five families, and this chapter takes them in order:

    1. Where can the data travel? Network isolation with VPC endpoints and endpoint policies.
    2. Who can read it? IAM policies scoped by resource and condition, and Lake Formation for table, column, and row permissions.
    3. Who did read it? CloudTrail and CloudWatch monitoring of data access, with alarms.
    4. What sensitive data is in it, and how do you hide it? Macie for data at rest in S3, Comprehend for text in flight, guardrails at the model boundary, and masking or tokenization strategies that keep the text useful.
    5. How long does it live? S3 Lifecycle rules and log retention.

    Bedrock's own privacy guarantees sit underneath all five, and the exam expects you to know what they cover and where they stop.

    Exam tip

    Read the noun in the question. "PII in documents stored in S3" points to Macie. "PII in text before it is sent to the model" or "redact transcripts in a pipeline" points to Comprehend. "PII in the model's response" or "mask PII in the prompt at inference time with no custom code" points to a Bedrock guardrail sensitive information filter. "Traffic must not traverse the public internet" points to an interface VPC endpoint. "Analysts may see only some columns or rows" points to Lake Formation. "Delete data after N days" points to an S3 Lifecycle rule.

    Network isolation for GenAI workloads (3.2.1)

    The Orientation chapter explained what a VPC is, why private subnets have no route to the internet, and how a VPC endpoint gives a private subnet a way to reach an AWS service (VPCs and subnets, VPC endpoints and PrivateLink). This section applies that to a GenAI application and adds the parts that the exam tests: which endpoints a Bedrock workload needs, how to restrict what flows through them, and how to make the private path mandatory.

    Which endpoints a Bedrock application needs

    A typical RAG or agent backend runs as a Lambda function or an ECS task in private subnets. When you attach a Lambda function to a VPC, Lambda places elastic network interfaces (ENIs, virtual network cards) in your subnets, and every call the function makes to an AWS API leaves through those ENIs. In a private subnet with no NAT gateway, a call to Bedrock simply times out unless an endpoint exists. Each AWS API your code calls needs its own endpoint.

    Your code callsEndpoint service nameEndpoint typeWhy
    converse, invoke_model, apply_guardrailcom.amazonaws.<region>.bedrock-runtimeInterfaceModel inference and standalone guardrail checks
    retrieve, retrieve_and_generate, invoke_agent, invoke_flowcom.amazonaws.<region>.bedrock-agent-runtimeInterfaceKnowledge base queries, agents, and flows at run time
    Creating knowledge bases, data sources, starting ingestioncom.amazonaws.<region>.bedrock-agentInterfaceBuild-time APIs, usually called from a pipeline
    Control plane: list models, guardrail configuration, logging settingscom.amazonaws.<region>.bedrockInterfaceRarely called from the application path
    OpenAI-compatible APIs on Bedrockcom.amazonaws.<region>.bedrock-mantleInterfaceOnly if you use the Responses or Chat Completions APIs. Its IAM actions use the bedrock-mantle: prefix, so endpoint policies for it differ from the bedrock: examples below
    Reading documents, writing outputscom.amazonaws.<region>.s3Gateway (free)S3 traffic through a route table entry
    Session state, token vaultcom.amazonaws.<region>.dynamodbGateway (free)Same mechanism as S3
    detect_pii_entitiescom.amazonaws.<region>.comprehendInterfacePII detection in the request path
    Decrypt with a customer managed key, generate_maccom.amazonaws.<region>.kmsInterfaceEnvelope encryption and tokenization
    Fetching an API key for a third-party toolcom.amazonaws.<region>.secretsmanagerInterfaceSecrets without internet egress

    Two details trip people up. First, Lambda function logs do not need a CloudWatch Logs endpoint, because the Lambda service ships them for you. Your own code calling logs:PutLogEvents or publishing custom metrics does need the logs or monitoring endpoint. Second, an interface endpoint has an hourly charge for each Availability Zone it is placed in, plus a per-GB processing charge, so ten endpoints across three Availability Zones is thirty billable endpoint-hours every hour. Gateway endpoints for S3 and DynamoDB have no charge. Check the AWS PrivateLink pricing page for your Region's numbers.

    With private DNS enabled on the endpoint (the default for AWS services), the normal hostname bedrock-runtime.<region>.amazonaws.com resolves to the endpoint's private IP addresses inside your VPC, so Boto3 code needs no change. Without private DNS, you pass the endpoint-specific URL as endpoint_url when you create the client.

    flowchart LR
  subgraph VPC["Your VPC (no internet gateway, no NAT)"]
    subgraph Priv["Private subnets in two AZs"]
      L["Lambda or ECS task (app role)"]
    end
    EPR["Interface endpoint: bedrock-runtime"]
    EPA["Interface endpoint: bedrock-agent-runtime"]
    EPC["Interface endpoint: comprehend, kms"]
    GW["Gateway endpoint: S3, DynamoDB"]
  end
  L -->|"converse, apply_guardrail"| EPR
  L -->|"retrieve"| EPA
  L -->|"detect_pii_entities, decrypt"| EPC
  L -->|"get_object, put_item"| GW
  EPR --> BR["Amazon Bedrock runtime"]
  EPA --> KB["Knowledge base"]
  KB -.->|"service role, Bedrock network"| VS["Vector store"]
  GW --> S3["S3 documents bucket"]
  KBR["KB ingestion (service role)"] -.->|"reads from AWS service network, not your VPC"| S3

    Follow one request. The Lambda function calls retrieve. DNS resolves the agent-runtime hostname to the endpoint's private address, so the request goes through the endpoint and never touches the internet. The knowledge base then queries its vector store using its own service role, from the Bedrock service's network. Your VPC endpoint does not carry that second hop. The same is true when the knowledge base ingests documents from S3: the read comes from Bedrock, not from your subnets. Keep that dashed line in mind, because it breaks a popular bucket policy later in this section.

    Backend analogy

    An interface endpoint is a sidecar proxy that lives inside your private network and has exactly one upstream. Private DNS is the /etc/hosts override that makes your existing client library talk to the sidecar without a config change. The endpoint policy is the proxy's allow list.

    Endpoint policies: what may pass through

    An endpoint policy is a resource-based policy attached to the endpoint. It is evaluated in addition to the caller's IAM policy, so a request needs an Allow from both. The default endpoint policy allows everything, which means an endpoint on its own gives privacy of the path but no restriction on what flows through it. Replace it with one that names the principals, actions, and resources you expect from this VPC.

    {
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Sid": "AppRoleInvokesApprovedModelsOnly",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Principal": { "AWS": "arn:aws:iam::111122223333:role/support-assistant-app" },
      "Action": [
        "bedrock:InvokeModel",
        "bedrock:InvokeModelWithResponseStream",
        "bedrock:ApplyGuardrail"
      ],
      "Resource": [
        "arn:aws:bedrock:*::foundation-model/anthropic.claude-*",
        "arn:aws:bedrock:eu-west-1:111122223333:inference-profile/*",
        "arn:aws:bedrock:eu-west-1:111122223333:guardrail/*"
      ]
    }
  ]
}

    With this policy, a developer who opens a shell on an instance in the same VPC with a different role cannot use the endpoint, and the app role cannot use it to call control-plane actions or other models. This is a useful data exfiltration control: even stolen credentials from another account cannot be used through your endpoint, because the principal is pinned to your role.

    Making the private path mandatory: aws:SourceVpce and aws:SourceVpc

    An endpoint policy controls traffic that uses the endpoint. It cannot stop a caller who goes around it, for example by running the same code on a laptop with the same role's credentials. To require the private path, the identity or resource policy has to check where the request came from. Two global condition keys carry that information, and both are present only when a request arrives through a VPC endpoint:

    Condition keyValueUse it to say
    aws:SourceVpceThe endpoint ID, for example vpce-0a1b2c3d4e5f60789"Only through this specific endpoint"
    aws:SourceVpcThe VPC ID, for example vpc-0123456789abcdef0"Only from this VPC, through any of its endpoints"
    aws:VpceOrgPaths / aws:VpceOrgIDThe organization path of the account that owns the endpoint"Only through endpoints owned by accounts in my organization"

    An identity policy statement on the app role that denies inference unless the call came through the endpoint looks like this:

    {
  "Sid": "DenyInferenceOutsideTheVpcEndpoint",
  "Effect": "Deny",
  "Action": [
    "bedrock:InvokeModel",
    "bedrock:InvokeModelWithResponseStream"
  ],
  "Resource": "*",
  "Condition": {
    "StringNotEquals": { "aws:SourceVpce": "vpce-0a1b2c3d4e5f60789" }
  }
}

    A negated operator such as StringNotEquals is true when the key is missing, so a call from the internet, where aws:SourceVpce does not exist, matches the Deny and is refused. Set-based operators behave differently: ForAnyValue evaluates to false on a missing key, which is why policies that use aws:VpceOrgPaths with ForAnyValue:StringNotLike need a second Deny with a Null check. The Domain 2 section on secure access covers the companion pattern, the bedrock:GuardrailIdentifier condition key that forces every inference call to use an approved guardrail.

    S3 bucket policies that require the endpoint, and the knowledge base trap

    The same idea applied to the documents bucket stops anyone from downloading the source material from outside the VPC. The trap is the dashed line in the diagram. A Bedrock knowledge base reads your bucket with its service role from the Bedrock service network, so its requests carry no aws:SourceVpce. A blanket Deny will make every ingestion job fail with access denied. Exempt the knowledge base service role by ARN:

    {
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Sid": "DenyObjectAccessOutsideVpcExceptKbRole",
      "Effect": "Deny",
      "Principal": "*",
      "Action": ["s3:GetObject", "s3:PutObject"],
      "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::acme-support-docs-111122223333/*",
      "Condition": {
        "StringNotEquals": {
          "aws:SourceVpce": "vpce-0fedcba9876543210"
        },
        "ArnNotEquals": {
          "aws:PrincipalArn": "arn:aws:iam::111122223333:role/service-role/AmazonBedrockExecutionRoleForKnowledgeBase_support"
        }
      }
    }
  ]
}

    Conditions in one block are combined with AND, so the Deny applies only when the request is outside the endpoint and the caller is not the knowledge base role. The statement deliberately covers object reads and writes only. If you also deny s3:PutBucketPolicy and s3:DeleteBucketPolicy outside the VPC, you can lock yourself out of the bucket, and recovery needs the account root user.

    Watch out

    A bucket policy keyed on aws:SourceIp does not work for traffic through a VPC endpoint, because the request then carries a private address. Use aws:SourceVpce or aws:SourceVpc for private traffic. Also remember that S3 gateway endpoints only serve requests from the same Region. A cross-Region bucket read from a private subnet still needs another path.

    Isolation for the other Bedrock pieces

    • Vector stores. An Amazon OpenSearch Serverless collection has a network policy that can make the collection private, reachable only from named VPC endpoints and, separately, from AWS services such as Bedrock. A knowledge base needs the service access, and your application needs the VPC access if it queries the collection directly. Aurora PostgreSQL with pgvector already lives in your VPC; the knowledge base reaches it through the RDS Data API with a secret in Secrets Manager.
    • Action group and tool Lambdas. A Lambda function that an agent calls can itself sit in a VPC to reach a private database. The agent invokes it through the Lambda service, so the function's resource-based policy, not your network, controls who may call it.
    • Custom model training. Model customization jobs accept a VPC configuration (subnets and security groups) so the job reads training data through your VPC. Check the current documentation for which job types support it.
    • Agents on AgentCore Runtime. AgentCore Runtime supports running agents with VPC connectivity so they can reach private resources. The details change quickly, so check the AgentCore documentation.

    Exam tip

    "A company requires that calls to Amazon Bedrock from its application never traverse the public internet" is answered by an interface VPC endpoint for bedrock-runtime (AWS PrivateLink), not by a NAT gateway (which uses the internet), a VPN (which connects your data center), or encryption in transit alone (which is always on). "Ensure only the approved application can use the endpoint" adds an endpoint policy. "Ensure the role cannot be used outside the VPC" adds a Deny with aws:SourceVpce.

    Secure data access patterns with IAM and Lake Formation (3.2.1)

    Network isolation answers "from where". Access control answers "who, to what". A GenAI workload touches more data stores than a typical API: the source documents, the vector index, conversation history, the prompt templates, the invocation logs, and often a data lake that agents query. Each needs its own scoped permission, and the exam rewards answers that keep those scopes separate.

    The four principals in a RAG workload

    Draw the roles before writing policies. Most mistakes come from one role doing two jobs.

    PrincipalNeedsMust not have
    App runtime role (Lambda or ECS task role)bedrock:InvokeModel on approved models, bedrock:Retrieve on one knowledge base, bedrock:ApplyGuardrail on one guardrail, read and write on its own DynamoDB session tables3:GetObject on the raw documents bucket (the knowledge base reads documents, the app does not), any bedrock:Create* or bedrock:Delete*
    Knowledge base service role (assumed by bedrock.amazonaws.com)s3:GetObject and s3:ListBucket on the curated documents prefix, the embedding model, the vector store, kms:Decrypt on the bucket keyAccess to the raw, unredacted bucket
    Ingestion pipeline role (Step Functions, Glue, or Lambda)Read raw, write curated, call Comprehend, start ingestion jobsModel invocation on production
    Auditor or security roleRead CloudTrail, read invocation logs (with kms:Decrypt on the log key)Invoking models, writing data

    The service role trust policy should carry confused deputy protection. A confused deputy is a service tricked into acting on your resources on behalf of someone else. The aws:SourceAccount and aws:SourceArn conditions say "Bedrock may assume this role only when acting for this account and this knowledge base":

    {
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{
    "Effect": "Allow",
    "Principal": { "Service": "bedrock.amazonaws.com" },
    "Action": "sts:AssumeRole",
    "Condition": {
      "StringEquals": { "aws:SourceAccount": "111122223333" },
      "ArnLike": { "aws:SourceArn": "arn:aws:bedrock:eu-west-1:111122223333:knowledge-base/*" }
    }
  }]
}

    Resource-level permissions for Bedrock resources

    Most Bedrock resources have ARNs, so policies can name them instead of using "Resource": "*". The app role policy for the support assistant looks like this. Read it as a list of exactly what the application may touch.

    {
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Sid": "InvokeApprovedModels",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": ["bedrock:InvokeModel", "bedrock:InvokeModelWithResponseStream"],
      "Resource": [
        "arn:aws:bedrock:*::foundation-model/anthropic.claude-*",
        "arn:aws:bedrock:eu-west-1:111122223333:inference-profile/eu.anthropic.*"
      ]
    },
    {
      "Sid": "QueryOneKnowledgeBase",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": ["bedrock:Retrieve"],
      "Resource": "arn:aws:bedrock:eu-west-1:111122223333:knowledge-base/KBSUPPORT01"
    },
    {
      "Sid": "UseOneGuardrail",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": ["bedrock:ApplyGuardrail"],
      "Resource": "arn:aws:bedrock:eu-west-1:111122223333:guardrail/gr0abc123def"
    },
    {
      "Sid": "OwnSessionTableOnly",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": ["dynamodb:GetItem", "dynamodb:PutItem", "dynamodb:Query"],
      "Resource": "arn:aws:dynamodb:eu-west-1:111122223333:table/support-sessions",
      "Condition": {
        "ForAllValues:StringEquals": { "dynamodb:LeadingKeys": ["${aws:PrincipalTag/tenant}"] }
      }
    }
  ]
}

    The last statement shows a pattern worth recognizing: dynamodb:LeadingKeys limits the role to items whose partition key equals a value from the caller's session tag, here a tenant ID. Combined with a per-tenant role session, it gives row-level isolation in DynamoDB without application logic. For knowledge bases, a common pattern is one knowledge base per sensitivity level, or metadata filters applied by a trusted backend. Do not let the browser send the filter, because a client can send a different one.

    Watch out

    There is no bedrock:Converse IAM action. Converse is authorized by bedrock:InvokeModel, and ConverseStream by bedrock:InvokeModelWithResponseStream. A policy that lists bedrock:Converse is silently ignored for that action and the call fails with AccessDeniedException. When you call through a cross-Region inference profile, allow the foundation model ARN in every Region the profile can route to (hence the * Region) plus the profile ARN itself.

    AWS Lake Formation: permissions on tables, columns, and rows

    IAM and S3 bucket policies work at the level of buckets, prefixes, and objects. That is too coarse when an agent or a text-to-SQL feature queries a data lake. A data lake on AWS usually means Parquet or CSV files in S3, described by tables in the AWS Glue Data Catalog (a metadata store that records each table's schema and S3 location, covered further in Chapter 3), and queried with Amazon Athena or Amazon Redshift Spectrum. Suppose a customers table has an email column and rows for three countries. You want the analytics assistant to query purchase totals, never see emails, and see only EU rows. An S3 policy cannot express any of that, because all the columns and rows sit in the same files.

    AWS Lake Formation is a service that layers database-style permissions on top of the Glue Data Catalog. You grant SELECT on a database, table, or set of columns to an IAM role, just as you would GRANT in PostgreSQL. When Athena, Redshift Spectrum, Amazon EMR, or AWS Glue runs the query, it asks Lake Formation for temporary credentials scoped to what the caller may read, and filters columns and rows before returning results. The S3 location is registered with Lake Formation, and from then on Lake Formation, not the caller's S3 permissions, decides access for integrated engines.

    Lake Formation featureWhat it doesGenAI example
    Named resource grantsGrant SELECT, DESCRIBE, and so on, on a database, table, or columnsThe text-to-SQL agent role gets SELECT on orders only
    Column-level permissionsInclude or exclude specific columnsExclude email and phone from the agent's grant
    Data filters (row-level and cell-level security)A row filter expression plus a column list, granted like a tablecountry IN ('DE','FR','IE') for the EU support assistant
    LF-Tags (tag-based access control)Attach tags such as sensitivity=pii to databases, tables, or columns, and grant on tag expressions"The GenAI roles may read anything tagged sensitivity=public," which covers new tables automatically
    Cross-account sharingShare catalog resources with another account through AWS RAMA central data account shares curated tables with the GenAI account

    The choice between Lake Formation and plain IAM depends on the shape of the data. Use IAM and bucket policies for unstructured documents that a knowledge base ingests, where the unit of access is a file or prefix. Use Lake Formation for structured tables queried through the Glue Data Catalog, where the unit of access is a column or row. An Amazon Bedrock knowledge base connected to a structured data store (it generates SQL against Amazon Redshift, including tables in the Glue Data Catalog) runs queries under a role you choose, so the same Lake Formation grants can limit what the generated SQL is able to return. Check the current documentation for which query engines and catalog configurations the structured knowledge base supports.

    Backend analogy

    Lake Formation is PostgreSQL's GRANT SELECT (col1, col2) ON table TO role plus row-level security policies, for a data lake made of files. LF-Tags are like granting on a schema label instead of listing tables, so a new table inherits the right permissions the moment it is tagged.

    Exam tip

    "Provide granular, column-level or row-level access to data in an S3 data lake for a GenAI application" is a Lake Formation question. Distractors are S3 bucket policies (object level only), IAM policies with s3:prefix conditions (still object level), and building separate copies of the table per audience (works, but adds storage, pipelines, and drift). "Manage permissions at scale as new tables are added" points to LF-Tags.

    Monitoring data access with CloudTrail and CloudWatch (3.2.1)

    Controls fail, so you need evidence of who read what. The Orientation chapter separated the three log types (CloudTrail, CloudWatch Logs, and model invocation logging). For data access monitoring, the important distinction is between CloudTrail management events, which are recorded by default, and data events, which you must turn on and which cost extra.

    ActivityCloudTrail categoryRecorded by default?
    InvokeModel, InvokeModelWithResponseStream, Converse, ConverseStreamManagement eventsYes (in Event history for 90 days; a trail for longer)
    Creating, updating, or deleting guardrails, knowledge bases, logging configurationManagement eventsYes
    Retrieve, RetrieveAndGenerate on a knowledge baseData events, resource type AWS::Bedrock::KnowledgeBaseNo, configure an advanced event selector
    InvokeAgent, InvokeFlowData events (AWS::Bedrock::AgentAlias, AWS::Bedrock::FlowAlias)No
    ApplyGuardrail, including guardrail evaluations during inferenceData events, AWS::Bedrock::GuardrailNo
    GetObject, PutObject on S3Data events, AWS::S3::ObjectNo

    CloudTrail records who called which API on which resource. It does not record prompt or response text. For the content of a conversation you need model invocation logging, and that brings the privacy concerns covered in the next section.

    From a log line to an alarm

    A trail can deliver events to a CloudWatch Logs log group. From there, a metric filter turns matching log lines into a CloudWatch metric, and a CloudWatch alarm notifies an Amazon SNS topic (a pub/sub notification service) when the metric crosses a threshold. Three filters cover the common data-access signals:

    # Variables: the log group your trail writes to, and an SNS topic for the security team
TRAIL_LG=aws-cloudtrail-logs-111122223333
TOPIC_ARN=arn:aws:sns:eu-west-1:111122223333:security-alerts

# 1. Access denied on Bedrock: someone probing models or knowledge bases they should not use
aws logs put-metric-filter --log-group-name "$TRAIL_LG" \
  --filter-name bedrock-access-denied \
  --filter-pattern '{ ($.eventSource = "bedrock.amazonaws.com") && (($.errorCode = "*AccessDenied*") || ($.errorCode = "*UnauthorizedOperation")) }' \
  --metric-transformations metricName=BedrockAccessDenied,metricNamespace=Security,metricValue=1

# 2. Someone weakened the safety or logging configuration
aws logs put-metric-filter --log-group-name "$TRAIL_LG" \
  --filter-name bedrock-config-weakened \
  --filter-pattern '{ ($.eventSource = "bedrock.amazonaws.com") && (($.eventName = "DeleteGuardrail") || ($.eventName = "DeleteModelInvocationLoggingConfiguration")) }' \
  --metric-transformations metricName=BedrockConfigWeakened,metricNamespace=Security,metricValue=1

# 3. Unusually many reads from the documents bucket (needs S3 data events on the trail)
aws logs put-metric-filter --log-group-name "$TRAIL_LG" \
  --filter-name docs-bucket-reads \
  --filter-pattern '{ ($.eventName = "GetObject") && ($.requestParameters.bucketName = "acme-support-docs-111122223333") }' \
  --metric-transformations metricName=DocsBucketReads,metricNamespace=Security,metricValue=1

aws cloudwatch put-metric-alarm --alarm-name bedrock-access-denied \
  --namespace Security --metric-name BedrockAccessDenied \
  --statistic Sum --period 300 --evaluation-periods 1 \
  --threshold 5 --comparison-operator GreaterThanOrEqualToThreshold \
  --treat-missing-data notBreaching --alarm-actions "$TOPIC_ARN"

    Two managed services add detection you do not have to write. Amazon GuardDuty analyzes CloudTrail events and can flag suspicious Bedrock activity, such as an unusual principal removing a guardrail. Amazon Macie evaluates S3 buckets continuously for public access and missing encryption, which the next section covers. Chapter 3 builds a fuller misuse and drift monitoring loop on top of these signals (continuous monitoring).

    Watch out

    S3 and Bedrock data events are charged per event recorded. A busy documents bucket or a chatty knowledge base can produce millions of events a day. Use advanced event selectors to limit data events to the specific bucket or knowledge base ARNs you care about, and check CloudTrail pricing before enabling them account-wide.

    What Amazon Bedrock does with your data (3.2.2)

    Before adding controls of your own, know what the platform already guarantees, because several exam distractors propose building something Bedrock already does. AWS describes Bedrock's data handling under the shared responsibility model: AWS protects the infrastructure and the service; you control your content, who can reach it, and how you configure the service.

    The guarantees

    • Your prompts and completions are not used to train the base models, and are not shared with model providers. This holds for every Bedrock model, including third-party models from Anthropic, Meta, Mistral, and others.
    • Model providers cannot see your traffic. In each Region, Bedrock runs each provider's model in a model deployment account owned and operated by the Bedrock service team. Bedrock makes a deep copy of the provider's inference and training software into that account. Providers have no access to those accounts, so they have no access to Bedrock logs, prompts, or completions.
    • Encryption in transit. All API traffic uses TLS. Bedrock requires TLS 1.2 and recommends TLS 1.3. FIPS 140-3 endpoints (for example bedrock-runtime-fips, also available as VPC endpoint service names) exist in a small set of US, Canadian, and GovCloud Regions for workloads that require them. Check the current endpoint list for your Region.
    • Encryption at rest. Resources Bedrock stores for you (custom models, agents, knowledge base ingestion data, guardrails, prompts, flows) are encrypted with AWS owned keys by default, and most accept a customer managed key (see the table below).
    • Fine-tuned models stay yours. A custom model trained on your data is private to your account. The training data is not used to improve the base model.
    • Regional processing. On-demand inference runs in the Region you call. A geographic cross-Region inference profile (prefix us., eu., apac., and similar) keeps processing within that geography. A global profile can route to any commercial Region, which matters for data residency rules (see Domain 2, cross-environment solutions).

    Data retention modes: a recent change to check

    Bedrock now exposes an explicit data retention mode, set per Region at the account level or per project. The modes form a scale from least to most permissive: none (zero data retention, nothing written to durable storage by AWS or shared with the provider), default (each model's standard policy applies; AWS may keep data for safety and abuse prevention, and the provider never receives it), and aws_review (AWS may retain inputs and outputs, for up to 30 days per the current documentation, for automated or human review that some providers require as a condition of access; the content stays inside AWS). A fourth value, provider_data_share, is marked legacy: the documentation states that Bedrock does not share content with model providers today, and new configurations should use aws_review. The value inherit means "no opinion at this scope", and it is the starting value for new accounts and projects, so the effective mode is the first non-inherit value of project, then account, then the model's own default. A model that requires a more permissive mode than yours is unavailable, and requests to it on bedrock-runtime fail with a ValidationException. Your mode sets the most retention you allow. It does not make a model retain data that the model can process without retention. An SCP (service control policy, an organization-wide permission guardrail) that denies bedrock:PutAccountDataRetention, bedrock:CreateProject, and bedrock:UpdateProject unless the bedrock:DataRetentionMode condition key equals none stops anyone in the organization from setting a more permissive mode. The OpenAI-compatible bedrock-mantle endpoint has the same keys under the bedrock-mantle: prefix. The keys control which mode can be set, not where retained data is stored. This feature is newer than the exam guide, so expect the exam to test the stable guarantees above, and check the Bedrock data retention documentation before you design around a specific mode.

    Customer managed keys across Bedrock

    The Orientation chapter explained AWS owned, AWS managed, and customer managed KMS keys (KMS keys and envelope encryption). Regulated workloads usually require a customer managed key (CMK), because only then do you own the key policy, see every use of the key in CloudTrail, and can revoke access by disabling the key. Where you attach it in Bedrock:

    Bedrock resourceWhere the key goesWhat it protects
    Knowledge base data sourceserverSideEncryptionConfiguration.kmsKeyArn on create_data_sourceTransient data while documents are parsed, chunked, and embedded
    Managed knowledge basemanagedKnowledgeBaseConfiguration.serverSideEncryptionConfiguration.kmsKeyArn on create_knowledge_base, set at creationTransient and stored data. Bedrock creates a KMS grant, so the identity that creates the knowledge base needs kms:CreateGrant on the key; the knowledge base service role needs no KMS permission for this key
    Knowledge base vector store (customer-managed knowledge base)Configured on the store itself (OpenSearch Serverless encryption policy, Aurora storage encryption, S3 Vectors encryption settings; check the current S3 Vectors documentation for the key options)The stored chunks and embeddings
    retrieve_and_generate sessionssessionConfiguration.kmsKeyArn on the requestThe session context for multi-turn answers
    Source documents in S3Bucket default encryption with SSE-KMSThe originals. The knowledge base role then needs kms:Decrypt
    Agents (classic, now in maintenance mode and closed to new customers)customerEncryptionKeyArn on create_agentAgent configuration and session data
    GuardrailskmsKeyId on create_guardrailDenied topics, word lists, regex patterns
    Prompt Management prompts and FlowscustomerEncryptionKeyArnTemplates and flow definitions
    Custom modelscustomModelKmsKeyId on create_model_customization_jobThe model artifacts. Training output in S3 uses the bucket's encryption
    Invocation logsKMS key on the CloudWatch Logs log group (associate-kms-key) and SSE-KMS on the S3 destinationPrompt and response text in logs

    Encryption with a CMK works only if every principal that reads the data has kms:Decrypt on the key, through the key policy or an IAM policy that the key policy allows. "AccessDenied" from a knowledge base ingestion job right after you switched the bucket to SSE-KMS almost always means the service role is missing kms:Decrypt. If you also set a key on the data source for transient ingestion data, the service role needs kms:GenerateDataKey and kms:Decrypt on that key too.

    Where the guarantees stop: invocation logs

    Bedrock does not keep your prompts for its own use, but you may. Model invocation logging is an account-level, per-Region setting that copies the full request and response of every inference call to CloudWatch Logs, S3, or both. It is off by default. It is extremely useful for debugging, evaluation, and audits. It is also the single most common place PII leaks in a Bedrock workload.

    Watch out

    A guardrail's PII masking changes what the API returns. When invocation logging is on, the original, unmasked content, including card numbers and national ID numbers in the user's prompt, can still be written to the log in plain text. Guardrail trace output, if you enable it, also echoes the matched sensitive text. For regulated workloads: encrypt the log group and log bucket with a customer managed key, restrict log reads to an auditor role, set a retention period, run Macie on the S3 log destination, and consider leaving text delivery off for the most sensitive workloads. Chapter 3 returns to token-level redaction in logs (audit and decision logs).

    Exam tip

    If an answer proposes "opt out of model training" or "sign an agreement so the provider does not see prompts", it is solving a problem Bedrock already solves. The exam's preferred privacy answers add controls on your side of the boundary: VPC endpoints, CMKs, scoped IAM, PII redaction, guardrails, and log protection.

    Detecting PII with Comprehend and Macie (3.2.2)

    You cannot protect sensitive data you have not found. AWS gives you three detectors, each built for a different place in the pipeline. They overlap in what they detect and differ in where they run, how they are priced, and what they do with a match.

    Amazon Comprehend: PII in text you are processing

    Amazon Comprehend is a managed natural language processing service. Its PII feature finds entities such as names, addresses, card numbers, and national ID numbers in English or Spanish text. It offers three operations:

    OperationModeReturnsUse it for
    contains_pii_entitiesReal time, one document up to 100 KBLabels: which PII types are present, with a score, but no locationsA fast yes or no gate: "does this ticket contain PII at all?"
    detect_pii_entitiesReal time, one document up to 100 KBEach entity's Type, Score, BeginOffset, EndOffsetMasking or tokenizing in your own code, in the request path
    start_pii_entities_detection_jobAsynchronous batch over S3 prefixesWith Mode="ONLY_OFFSETS", JSON offsets. With Mode="ONLY_REDACTION", a redacted copy of each document (a plain-text file named like the input with .out appended)Cleaning a corpus before indexing it into a knowledge base

    The real-time call gives you offsets, and your code decides what to do with each span. Sort the entities by offset in reverse order before replacing, so earlier replacements do not shift later offsets:

    import boto3

comprehend = boto3.client("comprehend", region_name="eu-west-1")

def redact(text: str, min_score: float = 0.8, keep: frozenset = frozenset({"DATE_TIME"})) -> str:
    """Replace each PII span with its entity type, for example [EMAIL]."""
    resp = comprehend.detect_pii_entities(Text=text, LanguageCode="en")
    entities = [
        e for e in resp["Entities"]
        if e["Score"] >= min_score and e["Type"] not in keep
    ]
    # Replace from the end of the string so earlier offsets stay valid
    for e in sorted(entities, key=lambda e: e["BeginOffset"], reverse=True):
        text = text[: e["BeginOffset"]] + f"[{e['Type']}]" + text[e["EndOffset"]:]
    return text

print(redact("Hi, I'm Paulo Santos, card 1111-0000-1111-0000, email paulo@example.com. "
             "The charge on 3 March is wrong."))
# Hi, I'm [NAME], card [CREDIT_DEBIT_NUMBER], email [EMAIL]. The charge on 3 March is wrong.

    Notice the keep set. Comprehend treats dates as PII (DATE_TIME), but the date is what the support agent needs. Deciding which entity types to remove is a business decision, and the best answer keeps the text useful for the model. Comprehend is priced per unit of 100 characters with a minimum charge per request, so even a very short ticket pays the minimum. Check the Comprehend pricing page for current rates.

    For a batch job, Comprehend writes redacted output itself. The RedactionConfig chooses entity types and the mask style: MaskMode="MASK" replaces each character with a mask character such as *, and MaskMode="REPLACE_WITH_PII_ENTITY_TYPE" writes the type name, which keeps more meaning for a model. The lab at the end of the chapter runs one.

    Amazon Macie: PII sitting in S3

    Amazon Macie is a data security service for S3. It does two jobs. First, once enabled, it continuously keeps an inventory of your buckets and evaluates their security: public access, encryption, sharing with other accounts. Problems become policy findings. Second, it inspects object contents with machine learning and pattern matching to find sensitive data, producing sensitive data findings. Content inspection runs in two ways:

    • Sensitive data discovery jobs that you create, either one time or scheduled (daily, weekly, monthly), over specific buckets, prefixes, or objects matching criteria such as tags or file types.
    • Automated sensitive data discovery, which samples objects across your whole S3 estate continuously and builds a sensitivity score per bucket. It is the cheaper way to answer "where in our 400 buckets is the PII?"; jobs are the way to answer "exactly which objects in this bucket contain it?"

    Macie uses managed data identifiers (built-in detectors for credentials, financial data, personal information, and health data, with country-specific variants) and custom data identifiers that you define with a regular expression plus optional keywords, for example an internal customer ID format such as CUST-\d{8}. Allow lists suppress known non-sensitive matches, such as your company's public support phone number.

    Macie publishes every new finding automatically to Amazon EventBridge (except findings that a suppression rule archives automatically), with source aws.macie and detail-type Macie Finding. That lets Macie act as a trigger as well as a report. The pattern that matters for GenAI is a quarantine gate in front of the knowledge base:

    flowchart LR
  Up["Documents uploaded to raw bucket"] --> Job["Macie scheduled job or automated discovery"]
  Job -->|"finding: SensitiveData:S3Object/Personal"| EB["EventBridge rule: source aws.macie"]
  EB --> Q["Lambda: tag object quarantine=true, notify data owner"]
  Q --> Red["Comprehend redaction job writes curated copy"]
  Red --> Cur["Curated bucket (KB data source)"]
  Cur --> Ing["KB ingestion job"]
  Job -->|"no sensitive data"| Cur

    An EventBridge rule that matches only high-severity personal data findings looks like this:

    {
  "source": ["aws.macie"],
  "detail-type": ["Macie Finding"],
  "detail": {
    "category": ["CLASSIFICATION"],
    "type": [{ "prefix": "SensitiveData:S3Object/" }],
    "severity": { "description": ["High"] }
  }
}

    Macie pricing has two parts: a monthly charge per bucket that Macie monitors, and a charge per GB of object data it inspects (with automated discovery charged per object monitored and per GB sampled). New Macie accounts get a 30-day free trial that covers bucket inventory and automated discovery, but not the discovery jobs you create yourself. Scanning a few test files costs cents. Scanning a multi-terabyte data lake with a full job is a real line item, so scope jobs with prefixes and include criteria. Check the Macie pricing page for current numbers.

    The guardrail sensitive information filter: PII at the model boundary

    The third detector sits inside Bedrock. A guardrail's sensitive information filter checks the prompt, the response, or both, at inference time, against built-in PII entity types and your own regular expressions. Chapter 1 described guardrails as a whole (anatomy of a guardrail). The PII part has two actions per entity type:

    • BLOCK: the guardrail intervenes and returns your blocked message instead of the content.
    • ANONYMIZE: the guardrail replaces the span with a placeholder such as {EMAIL} and lets the rest through.

    You can choose per entity type, for example block card numbers but anonymize emails. The API also lets you set the action separately for input and output (inputAction and outputAction, each BLOCK, ANONYMIZE, or NONE to detect without acting), and switch evaluation off for one side with inputEnabled or outputEnabled. The same fields exist on regex entries. Guardrail entity type names are not the same as Comprehend's: Comprehend says CREDIT_DEBIT_NUMBER and SSN, a guardrail says CREDIT_DEBIT_CARD_NUMBER and US_SOCIAL_SECURITY_NUMBER. Do not copy lists between the two.

    Choosing the detector

    Amazon MacieAmazon Comprehend PIIBedrock guardrail sensitive information filter
    Where the data isAt rest in S3Text you pass to it, in flight or in batch from S3The prompt and the response of a model call
    What it returnsFindings (bucket, object, type, count, location), sent to EventBridge and Security HubEntity offsets, labels, or redacted copiesMasked or blocked content, plus an assessment
    Changes the data?No, it reportsBatch jobs write a redacted copy; real time returns offsets for your codeYes, in the API response
    Custom patternsCustom data identifiers (regex plus keywords), allow listsEntity type selection, custom entity recognizers (separate feature)Regex filters
    Languages and formatsMany file formats: text, CSV, JSON, Parquet, PDF, Office documents, archivesPlain text, English and SpanishText in the conversation; check the docs for language coverage
    Typical GenAI jobFind PII in documents before they are indexed, or in exported invocation logsRedact transcripts before indexing, pseudonymize prompts before the model sees themLast line of defense: stop PII entering or leaving the model, with no custom code

    A mature system uses all three, one per layer. Macie tells you where PII exists at rest. Comprehend removes it in the pipeline. The guardrail catches what slipped through at inference time. That layering is the "defense in depth" Chapter 1 described for harmful content (defense in depth), applied to privacy.

    Exam tip

    Macie only reads S3. An answer that uses Macie to scan a DynamoDB table, a live prompt, or an RDS database is wrong. Comprehend PII detection is English and Spanish only. If a scenario needs PII masking in the model response "without writing code", the answer is the guardrail; if it needs a redacted copy of a corpus before indexing, the answer is a Comprehend batch redaction job.

    Masking, pseudonymization, and anonymization that keep the model useful (3.2.3)

    Detecting PII is half the job. The other half is deciding what to replace it with, and that decision sets how useful the text still is to a model. Strip every name and number from a support ticket and the model cannot tell the customer which order is late. Leave them in and you send personal data to places it does not need to go. Skill 3.2.3 asks you to protect privacy while maintaining FM utility, so the exam wants you to know the techniques and their trade-offs.

    The four techniques

    TechniqueWhat happens to "Paulo Santos, card 1111-0000-1111-0000"Reversible?Model utilityFits
    Redaction (masking)***** ******, card *******************NoLow: the model loses even the fact that a card was mentionedLogs and archives no one needs to read
    Type substitution[NAME], card [CREDIT_DEBIT_NUMBER]NoMedium: the model knows a name and a card existKnowledge base corpora, analytics, evaluation datasets
    Pseudonymization (tokenization)<NAME_7f3a90e1>, card <CREDIT_DEBIT_NUMBER_91bc04de>, with the mapping stored in a secure vaultYes, for authorized callersHigh: the same person gets the same token everywhere, so the model can reason about "the same customer" across turns and documentsLive assistants that must answer with the real value at the end
    Anonymization (generalization)a customer in Lisbon, aged 30 to 39, card ending 0000NoMedium to high for aggregate questionsDatasets for fine-tuning, analytics, sharing with other teams

    The legal distinction matters for the exam's compliance scenarios. Under GDPR and similar laws, pseudonymized data is still personal data, because someone holds the key to reverse it. Truly anonymized data, where no one can re-identify the person with reasonable effort, falls outside many of those rules. Removing direct identifiers is often not enough for anonymity: a combination such as postcode, birth date, and gender can identify most people. Formal techniques such as k-anonymity (every record is indistinguishable from at least k minus 1 others on those quasi-identifiers) address that, but they are a specialist topic and the exam treats them at the level of "anonymization strategies". If a scenario needs proven anonymity, the safer answer involves the data protection team, not only a Lambda function.

    How a token vault works

    Pseudonymization is the technique that keeps the most utility, and it is the one you will build in a real assistant. The flow, for a user who types "Refund the double charge on card 1111-0000-1111-0000 for Paulo Santos":

    sequenceDiagram
  participant U as User
  participant A as App (Lambda)
  participant C as Comprehend
  participant K as KMS (HMAC key)
  participant V as DynamoDB vault
  participant B as Bedrock (with guardrail)
  U->>A: Refund card 1111-0000-1111-0000 for Paulo Santos
  A->>C: detect_pii_entities
  C-->>A: NAME and CREDIT_DEBIT_NUMBER spans with offsets
  A->>K: generate_mac for each value
  K-->>A: deterministic MACs
  A->>V: put_item token to value (encrypted, TTL 24h)
  A->>B: converse(Refund card CREDIT_DEBIT_NUMBER_91bc04de for NAME_7f3a90e1)
  B-->>A: I have refunded card CREDIT_DEBIT_NUMBER_91bc04de for NAME_7f3a90e1.
  A->>V: get_item for each token in the reply
  A-->>U: I have refunded card ending 0000 for Paulo Santos.

    Three design choices make this work:

    1. Deterministic tokens. The token is derived from the value with a keyed hash, so "Paulo Santos" always becomes NAME_7f3a90e1 within a session or tenant. AWS KMS supports HMAC keys, and generate_mac computes the hash without the key ever leaving KMS. A plain SHA-256 of a card number is not safe, because the space of card numbers is small enough to brute-force; the secret key prevents that.
    2. The vault is the only place the real value lives. A DynamoDB table encrypted with a customer managed key, readable only by the detokenizing step, with a TTL attribute so entries expire automatically.
    3. Detokenize only where the policy allows. The final reply to the verified customer gets real values (or partial ones, like "card ending 0000"). The invocation log, the analytics stream, and the evaluation dataset keep the tokens.
    import re
import time
import boto3

REGION = "eu-west-1"
HMAC_KEY_ID = "alias/pii-tokenizer"   # KMS key with KeySpec HMAC_256, KeyUsage GENERATE_VERIFY_MAC
VAULT_TABLE = "pii-vault"             # partition key: token (S); TTL attribute: expires_at
TOKENIZE = {"NAME", "EMAIL", "PHONE", "CREDIT_DEBIT_NUMBER", "ADDRESS"}

comprehend = boto3.client("comprehend", region_name=REGION)
kms = boto3.client("kms", region_name=REGION)
vault = boto3.resource("dynamodb", region_name=REGION).Table(VAULT_TABLE)


def _token(entity_type: str, value: str, tenant: str) -> str:
    mac = kms.generate_mac(
        KeyId=HMAC_KEY_ID,
        Message=f"{tenant}|{entity_type}|{value}".encode(),
        MacAlgorithm="HMAC_SHA_256",
    )["Mac"]
    return f"<{entity_type}_{mac.hex()[:8]}>"


def pseudonymize(text: str, tenant: str, ttl_seconds: int = 86400) -> str:
    entities = comprehend.detect_pii_entities(Text=text, LanguageCode="en")["Entities"]
    for e in sorted(entities, key=lambda e: e["BeginOffset"], reverse=True):
        if e["Type"] not in TOKENIZE or e["Score"] < 0.8:
            continue
        value = text[e["BeginOffset"]:e["EndOffset"]]
        token = _token(e["Type"], value, tenant)
        vault.put_item(Item={
            "token": token,
            "tenant": tenant,
            "value": value,                       # table is encrypted with a customer managed key
            "expires_at": int(time.time()) + ttl_seconds,
        })
        text = text[: e["BeginOffset"]] + token + text[e["EndOffset"]:]
    return text


def detokenize(text: str, tenant: str) -> str:
    def swap(match: re.Match) -> str:
        item = vault.get_item(Key={"token": match.group(0)}).get("Item")
        if not item or item["tenant"] != tenant:
            return match.group(0)               # unknown or foreign token: leave it masked
        if match.group(1) == "CREDIT_DEBIT_NUMBER":
            return "card ending " + item["value"][-4:]
        return item["value"]
    return re.sub(r"<([A-Z_]+)_[0-9a-f]{8}>", swap, text)

    Tell the model in the system prompt that tokens such as <NAME_7f3a90e1> are placeholders to be repeated exactly and never guessed. In practice models handle this well, and a guardrail with ANONYMIZE on the output is a backstop if the model ever produces a real-looking value on its own.

    Guardrail PII handling on input versus output

    The guardrail's sensitive information filter gives you masking without code, with one important difference between input and output:

    • On input, BLOCK refuses the whole request. ANONYMIZE on input means the model sees the placeholder instead of the value, which protects the model call but also means the model cannot use the value. Use it when the value is never needed for the answer.
    • On output, ANONYMIZE is the common choice: the user sees {EMAIL} where the model would have repeated an email address from a retrieved document. BLOCK on output suits zero-tolerance types such as national ID numbers.
    • Streaming caveat. With ConverseStream in asynchronous guardrail mode, chunks reach the user before the guardrail finishes, and PII masking is not applied. Use synchronous mode for PII-sensitive streams.

    Backend analogy

    A token vault is the same pattern as payment card tokenization in a PCI-compliant checkout: your services pass around tok_91bc04de, and only the vault service, behind its own access policy, can turn it back into a card number. The difference here is that the "service" handling the token is a language model.

    Exam tip

    "Mask PII but allow the application to restore the original values for authorized users" is pseudonymization with a secured mapping, not redaction and not a guardrail alone (a guardrail cannot reverse its own masking). "Prepare a dataset for fine-tuning that must not contain personal data" points to anonymization or type substitution in a batch pipeline, usually Comprehend, before the data ever reaches the customization job.

    Data retention with S3 Lifecycle and log retention (3.2.2)

    Privacy laws include storage limitation: keep personal data only as long as you need it. A GenAI system accumulates copies quickly: raw uploads, redacted copies, the vector index, conversation history, invocation logs, evaluation datasets. Every copy needs an end date. The cheapest way to delete data on time is to let the storage service do it.

    S3 Lifecycle rules

    An S3 Lifecycle configuration is a set of rules on a bucket. Each rule has a filter (a prefix, object tags, or object size) and actions that S3 runs automatically as objects age:

    • Transition objects to a cheaper storage class after N days, for example S3 Standard-IA, S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval, or S3 Glacier Deep Archive.
    • Expire objects after N days, which deletes them.
    • Expire noncurrent versions on a versioned bucket. With versioning on, "deleting" an object only adds a delete marker and the old version stays (and is billed). NoncurrentVersionExpiration removes old versions after N days, and ExpiredObjectDeleteMarker cleans up markers left behind.
    • Abort incomplete multipart uploads, which otherwise sit invisibly and cost money.
    flowchart LR
  D0["Day 0: raw ticket export lands in raw/"] --> D1["Day 1: redacted copy written to curated/"]
  D1 --> D7["Day 7: raw/ objects expire (rule raw-7d)"]
  D7 --> D30["Day 30: curated/ moves to Standard-IA"]
  D30 --> D365["Day 365: curated/ expires; noncurrent versions expire 30 days later"]
    {
  "Rules": [
    {
      "ID": "raw-uploads-expire-7d",
      "Filter": { "Prefix": "raw/" },
      "Status": "Enabled",
      "Expiration": { "Days": 7 },
      "NoncurrentVersionExpiration": { "NoncurrentDays": 1 },
      "AbortIncompleteMultipartUpload": { "DaysAfterInitiation": 1 }
    },
    {
      "ID": "curated-ia-then-expire",
      "Filter": { "Prefix": "curated/" },
      "Status": "Enabled",
      "Transitions": [{ "Days": 30, "StorageClass": "STANDARD_IA" }],
      "Expiration": { "Days": 365 },
      "NoncurrentVersionExpiration": { "NoncurrentDays": 30 }
    },
    {
      "ID": "invocation-logs-expire-90d",
      "Filter": { "Prefix": "AWSLogs/" },
      "Status": "Enabled",
      "Expiration": { "Days": 90 }
    }
  ]
}

    Lifecycle actions run asynchronously, so an object may still be visible for a short time after its expiry date, though you stop paying for it from that date. Storage classes have minimum storage durations (30 days for Standard-IA, 90 for Glacier Flexible Retrieval, 180 for Glacier Deep Archive), and transitioning an object you then delete early bills the remainder. Check the S3 pricing page when you design transitions.

    Deleting from S3 does not delete from the knowledge base. When a source document expires, the chunks and embeddings stay in the vector store until the next ingestion job (sync) notices the document is gone and removes them. Schedule a sync after the expiry window, and treat the vector store, the conversation history table (DynamoDB TTL), and the log groups as separate stores, each with its own retention setting.

    Logs and the opposite requirement

    CloudWatch Logs log groups keep data forever by default. Set a retention period on every log group that may hold prompts or responses:

    aws logs put-retention-policy --log-group-name /aws/bedrock/invocations --retention-in-days 90
aws logs associate-kms-key --log-group-name /aws/bedrock/invocations \
  --kms-key-id arn:aws:kms:eu-west-1:111122223333:key/1234abcd-12ab-34cd-56ef-1234567890ab

    Some rules require the opposite: records that must not be deleted or changed for a fixed time, such as audit logs for financial advice. S3 Object Lock stores objects in a write-once-read-many (WORM) mode with a retention period. In compliance mode no one, including the root user, can delete the object until the period ends; in governance mode users with a special permission can. Object Lock and Lifecycle work together: Lifecycle can only expire an object once its lock has passed. Chapter 3 uses Object Lock for audit trails (audit and decision logs).

    Exam tip

    "Automatically delete conversation transcripts stored in S3 after 30 days" is an S3 Lifecycle expiration rule, not a scheduled Lambda that lists and deletes objects (more code, more cost, more failure modes). "Must retain records unaltered for seven years" is S3 Object Lock in compliance mode. "Old versions still consume storage after deletion" is a missing NoncurrentVersionExpiration.

    Hands-on labs

    Each lab shows the Console path first and the CLI or Boto3 path second. Run them in the Region your project uses, and replace 111122223333 and the bucket names with your own. Use only fake test data: the sample values below are well-known test numbers, not real people. Labs 2.1, 2.3, and 2.4 cost cents or nothing. Lab 2.2 enables Macie, which charges per bucket and per GB inspected, so disable it at the end unless you plan to keep using it.

    Hands-on lab

    Lab 2.1: Redact PII from documents before they reach a knowledge base

    About 30 minutes · Cost: cents (Comprehend charges per 100 characters) · Needs: permission to create S3 buckets, IAM roles, and Comprehend jobs

    You will put three fake support transcripts in a raw/ prefix, run a Comprehend redaction job that writes cleaned copies to curated/, and compare them. The curated prefix is what a knowledge base data source would point at.

    Console path
    1. On your machine, create t1.txt with the text Customer Paulo Santos (paulo.santos@example.com, +1 206 555 0100) says card 4111 1111 1111 1111 was charged twice on 3 March. Create t2.txt and t3.txt with similar made-up details.
    2. Open Services → S3 → Create bucket. Name it lab32-pii-111122223333, keep Block all Public Access on, and create it. Open the bucket, choose Create folder and create raw/, then Upload the three files into raw/.
    3. Open Services → Amazon Comprehend → Analysis jobs → Create job. Name lab32-redact. Analysis type: Personally identifiable information (PII). Language: English.
    4. Under Output mode choose Redactions. Under PII entity types, select NAME, EMAIL, PHONE, and CREDIT_DEBIT_NUMBER (leave DATE_TIME unselected so the date survives). For the redaction style choose Replace with PII entity type.
    5. Under Input data, choose My documents, enter s3://lab32-pii-111122223333/raw/, and set Input format to One document per file. Under Output data, enter s3://lab32-pii-111122223333/curated/.
    6. Under Access permissions, choose Create an IAM role, keep the permissions scoped to the input and output locations, and give the role the name suffix lab32. Choose Create job.
    7. Wait until the job status is Completed (usually a few minutes). Open the S3 bucket, browse into curated/ (Comprehend adds a folder named after the account and job ID) and open one output file. You should see Customer [NAME] ([EMAIL], [PHONE]) says card [CREDIT_DEBIT_NUMBER] was charged twice on 3 March.
    8. For a real-time check, open Comprehend → Real-time analysis, choose Built-in, paste one transcript, and choose Analyze. Open the PII tab and compare the offsets and confidence scores with what the batch job removed.
    CLI and Boto3 path
    1. Create the bucket and upload test files: 
      export AWS_REGION=<your-region>
export ACCOUNT_ID=$(aws sts get-caller-identity --query Account --output text)
export BUCKET=lab32-pii-$ACCOUNT_ID

aws s3 mb s3://$BUCKET
for i in 1 2 3; do
  echo "Customer Paulo Santos (paulo.santos@example.com, +1 206 555 0100) says card 4111 1111 1111 1111 was charged twice on 3 March. Ticket $i." > t$i.txt
  aws s3 cp t$i.txt s3://$BUCKET/raw/t$i.txt
done
    2. Create the data access role that Comprehend assumes. It may read raw/ and write curated/, nothing else: 
      cat > trust.json <<EOF
{"Version":"2012-10-17","Statement":[{"Effect":"Allow",
 "Principal":{"Service":"comprehend.amazonaws.com"},"Action":"sts:AssumeRole",
 "Condition":{"StringEquals":{"aws:SourceAccount":"$ACCOUNT_ID"}}}]}
EOF
cat > perms.json <<EOF
{"Version":"2012-10-17","Statement":[
 {"Effect":"Allow","Action":"s3:GetObject","Resource":"arn:aws:s3:::$BUCKET/raw/*"},
 {"Effect":"Allow","Action":"s3:ListBucket","Resource":"arn:aws:s3:::$BUCKET"},
 {"Effect":"Allow","Action":"s3:PutObject","Resource":"arn:aws:s3:::$BUCKET/curated/*"}]}
EOF
aws iam create-role --role-name lab32-comprehend --assume-role-policy-document file://trust.json
aws iam put-role-policy --role-name lab32-comprehend --policy-name s3 --policy-document file://perms.json
export ROLE_ARN=arn:aws:iam::$ACCOUNT_ID:role/lab32-comprehend
    3. Start the redaction job (wait about ten seconds after creating the role, because IAM changes take a moment to propagate): 
      JOB_ID=$(aws comprehend start-pii-entities-detection-job \
  --job-name lab32-redact \
  --language-code en \
  --mode ONLY_REDACTION \
  --redaction-config 'PiiEntityTypes=NAME,EMAIL,PHONE,CREDIT_DEBIT_NUMBER,MaskMode=REPLACE_WITH_PII_ENTITY_TYPE' \
  --input-data-config "S3Uri=s3://$BUCKET/raw/,InputFormat=ONE_DOC_PER_FILE" \
  --output-data-config "S3Uri=s3://$BUCKET/curated/" \
  --data-access-role-arn $ROLE_ARN \
  --query JobId --output text)

# Poll until COMPLETED
aws comprehend describe-pii-entities-detection-job --job-id $JOB_ID \
  --query 'PiiEntitiesDetectionJobProperties.JobStatus' --output text

# Comprehend writes into a subfolder named after the account and job; each output file is the input name plus .out
aws s3 cp s3://$BUCKET/curated/ ./curated --recursive
find curated -name 't1*' -exec cat {} \;
    4. Run the real-time version from Python with the redact function from the PII detection section, and try changing min_score to 0.99 to see which entities have lower confidence.

    Cleanup. Console: empty and delete the bucket (S3 → bucket → Empty, then Delete), and delete the Comprehend data access role under IAM → Roles. CLI: aws s3 rb s3://$BUCKET --force, then aws iam delete-role-policy --role-name lab32-comprehend --policy-name s3 and aws iam delete-role --role-name lab32-comprehend. Completed Comprehend jobs cost nothing to keep.

    Hands-on lab

    Lab 2.2: Find PII at rest with a one-time Macie job, and route findings to EventBridge

    About 40 minutes · Cost: cents for a few small files, plus the per-bucket monthly charge pro-rated while Macie is enabled (a 30-day free trial for new Macie accounts covers bucket inventory and automated discovery but not jobs you create; check the Macie pricing page) · Needs: permission to enable Macie and create EventBridge rules and SNS topics

    You will enable Macie, scan a test bucket that contains fake PII, read the finding, and wire an EventBridge rule that emails you when Macie finds personal data. That rule is the trigger for the quarantine pattern in this chapter.

    Console path
    1. Create a bucket lab32-macie-111122223333 and upload a CSV file customers.csv with a header row name,email,ssn,card and a few rows of fake values such as Jane Doe,jane@example.com,123-45-6789,4111111111111111. Also upload one text file with no personal data.
    2. Open Services → Amazon Macie → Get started → Enable Macie. If Macie offers to turn on automated sensitive data discovery, you can turn it off for this lab under Settings → Automated discovery to keep costs to the job only.
    3. Macie may ask you to configure a repository for sensitive data discovery results (an S3 bucket and a KMS key). You configure it under Settings → Discovery results, or with the put-classification-export-configuration CLI command. For a short lab you can skip it: findings still appear, but Macie stores the detailed per-object results for only 90 days and you cannot read them directly without a repository. In production, configure it so you keep the evidence.
    4. Open Services → Amazon SNS → Topics → Create topic, type Standard, name lab32-macie-alerts. Choose Create subscription, protocol Email, your address, and confirm the email you receive.
    5. Open Services → Amazon EventBridge → Rules → Create rule. Name lab32-macie-pii, event bus default, Rule with an event pattern. Choose Custom pattern (JSON editor) and paste the pattern from the Macie section, but remove the severity block so any sensitive data finding matches. Target: SNS topic → lab32-macie-alerts. Create the rule.
