    6. Back in Macie, open Jobs → Create job. Select lab32-macie-111122223333, choose Next, review, and on Refine the scope choose One-time job. For Managed data identifiers choose Recommended. Skip custom identifiers and allow lists. Name the job lab32-scan and Submit.
    7. Wait for the job status to show Complete (often 10 to 20 minutes, mostly start-up time). Open Findings. You should see a finding such as SensitiveData:S3Object/Multiple or SensitiveData:S3Object/Personal for customers.csv, and none for the clean file. Open it to see the detected types, counts, and cell locations.
    8. Check your email for the EventBridge notification carrying the same finding as JSON.
    CLI and Boto3 path
    1. Create the bucket and data, enable Macie, and create the alert path: 
      export MACIE_BUCKET=lab32-macie-$ACCOUNT_ID
aws s3 mb s3://$MACIE_BUCKET
printf 'name,email,ssn,card\nJane Doe,jane@example.com,123-45-6789,4111111111111111\nJohn Roe,john@example.com,987-65-4320,5555555555554444\n' > customers.csv
echo "Our office opens at 9am." > clean.txt
aws s3 cp customers.csv s3://$MACIE_BUCKET/
aws s3 cp clean.txt s3://$MACIE_BUCKET/

aws macie2 enable-macie

TOPIC_ARN=$(aws sns create-topic --name lab32-macie-alerts --query TopicArn --output text)
aws sns subscribe --topic-arn $TOPIC_ARN --protocol email --notification-endpoint you@example.com
# Allow EventBridge to publish to the topic
aws sns set-topic-attributes --topic-arn $TOPIC_ARN --attribute-name Policy --attribute-value "{
  \"Version\":\"2012-10-17\",\"Statement\":[{\"Effect\":\"Allow\",
  \"Principal\":{\"Service\":\"events.amazonaws.com\"},\"Action\":\"sns:Publish\",\"Resource\":\"$TOPIC_ARN\",
  \"Condition\":{\"ArnEquals\":{\"aws:SourceArn\":\"arn:aws:events:$AWS_REGION:$ACCOUNT_ID:rule/lab32-macie-pii\"}}}]}"

aws events put-rule --name lab32-macie-pii --event-pattern '{
  "source": ["aws.macie"],
  "detail-type": ["Macie Finding"],
  "detail": { "category": ["CLASSIFICATION"] }
}'
aws events put-targets --rule lab32-macie-pii --targets "Id=sns,Arn=$TOPIC_ARN"
    2. Create the one-time job and wait for it: 
      JOB_ID=$(aws macie2 create-classification-job \
  --job-type ONE_TIME \
  --name lab32-scan \
  --managed-data-identifier-selector RECOMMENDED \
  --s3-job-definition "{\"bucketDefinitions\":[{\"accountId\":\"$ACCOUNT_ID\",\"buckets\":[\"$MACIE_BUCKET\"]}]}" \
  --query jobId --output text)

aws macie2 describe-classification-job --job-id $JOB_ID --query jobStatus --output text   # until COMPLETE
    3. Read the findings from Boto3: 
      import boto3

macie = boto3.client("macie2")
ids = macie.list_findings(
    findingCriteria={"criterion": {"category": {"eq": ["CLASSIFICATION"]}}}
)["findingIds"]
for f in macie.get_findings(findingIds=ids[:50])["findings"]:
    obj = f["resourcesAffected"]["s3Object"]["key"]
    kinds = [
        d["type"]
        for cat in f["classificationDetails"]["result"]["sensitiveData"]
        for d in cat["detections"]
    ]
    print(f["severity"]["description"], f["type"], obj, kinds)
# High SensitiveData:S3Object/Multiple customers.csv ['USA_SOCIAL_SECURITY_NUMBER', 'NAME', 'CREDIT_CARD_NUMBER', ...]

    Cleanup. Console: Macie → Settings → Disable Macie (this deletes Macie's findings and configuration in the Region; suspending keeps them but also stops charges), delete the EventBridge rule, the SNS topic, and the bucket. CLI: aws macie2 disable-macie, aws events remove-targets --rule lab32-macie-pii --ids sns, aws events delete-rule --name lab32-macie-pii, aws sns delete-topic --topic-arn $TOPIC_ARN, aws s3 rb s3://$MACIE_BUCKET --force.

    Hands-on lab

    Lab 2.3: Enforce retention with an S3 Lifecycle rule and a log retention policy

    About 15 minutes · Cost: none · Needs: permission to manage S3 buckets and CloudWatch Logs

    Lifecycle rules act on days, so you cannot watch an object expire in a 15-minute lab. You can confirm that S3 has scheduled the expiry: once a rule applies, a HEAD request on the object returns the expiry date and the rule ID.

    Console path
    1. Create a bucket lab32-retention-111122223333 and open Properties → Bucket Versioning → Edit → Enable, so you can see the noncurrent-version rule matter.
    2. Upload a file into a raw/ folder, then upload a changed file with the same name, so the first upload becomes a noncurrent version. Turn on Show versions to see both.
    3. Open Management → Lifecycle rules → Create lifecycle rule. Name raw-uploads-expire-7d. Choose Limit the scope of this rule using one or more filters and enter prefix raw/.
    4. Under Lifecycle rule actions, select Expire current versions of objects, Permanently delete noncurrent versions of objects, and Delete expired object delete markers or incomplete multipart uploads. Enter 7 days for expiry, 1 day for noncurrent versions, and 1 day for incomplete multipart uploads. Review the timeline summary at the bottom and choose Create rule.
    5. Open the object's detail page. After a short delay, the Object overview shows an Expiration date and Expiration rule.
    6. Open Services → CloudWatch → Log groups, choose any log group from an earlier lab (or create /lab32/prompts), choose Actions → Edit retention setting(s), and set 90 days.
    CLI and Boto3 path
    1. Create the bucket, enable versioning, and upload two versions: 
      export RET_BUCKET=lab32-retention-$ACCOUNT_ID
aws s3 mb s3://$RET_BUCKET
aws s3api put-bucket-versioning --bucket $RET_BUCKET --versioning-configuration Status=Enabled
echo "v1" > ticket.txt && aws s3 cp ticket.txt s3://$RET_BUCKET/raw/ticket.txt
echo "v2" > ticket.txt && aws s3 cp ticket.txt s3://$RET_BUCKET/raw/ticket.txt
aws s3api list-object-versions --bucket $RET_BUCKET --prefix raw/ --query 'Versions[].{Key:Key,Latest:IsLatest,Id:VersionId}'
    2. Apply the rule. Save the first rule from the retention section as lifecycle.json inside a {"Rules": [ ... ]} wrapper, then: 
      aws s3api put-bucket-lifecycle-configuration --bucket $RET_BUCKET \
  --lifecycle-configuration file://lifecycle.json
aws s3api get-bucket-lifecycle-configuration --bucket $RET_BUCKET

# Confirm S3 has scheduled the expiry (may take a few minutes to appear)
aws s3api head-object --bucket $RET_BUCKET --key raw/ticket.txt --query Expiration
# "expiry-date=\"Fri, 04 Dec 2026 00:00:00 GMT\", rule-id=\"raw-uploads-expire-7d\""
    3. Set log retention: 
      aws logs create-log-group --log-group-name /lab32/prompts
aws logs put-retention-policy --log-group-name /lab32/prompts --retention-in-days 90
aws logs describe-log-groups --log-group-name-prefix /lab32 --query 'logGroups[].[logGroupName,retentionInDays]'

    Cleanup. Console: on a versioned bucket, Empty removes all versions; then Delete the bucket, and delete the /lab32/prompts log group. CLI: delete every version first, because rb --force does not remove noncurrent versions on a versioned bucket: aws s3api delete-objects --bucket $RET_BUCKET --delete "$(aws s3api list-object-versions --bucket $RET_BUCKET --query '{Objects: Versions[].{Key:Key,VersionId:VersionId}}' --output json)", repeat with DeleteMarkers in place of Versions if any exist, then aws s3 rb s3://$RET_BUCKET and aws logs delete-log-group --log-group-name /lab32/prompts.

    Hands-on lab

    Lab 2.4: Allow document reads only through a VPC endpoint

    About 40 minutes · Cost: none for the S3 gateway endpoint and CloudShell; an optional interface endpoint step is charged per hour per Availability Zone · Needs: a VPC with at least one subnet (the default VPC works), permission to create VPC endpoints and edit bucket policies

    You will create a free S3 gateway endpoint, add a bucket policy that denies GetObject unless the request arrives through that endpoint, and prove it from two shells: AWS CloudShell on the public internet (denied) and a CloudShell VPC environment inside your VPC (allowed). The policy only denies object reads and writes, so you can always remove it.

    Console path
    1. Create a bucket lab32-vpce-111122223333 and upload doc.txt.
    2. Open Services → VPC → Endpoints → Create endpoint. Name lab32-s3-gw. Type: AWS services. Search s3 and choose com.amazonaws.<region>.s3 with type Gateway. Select your VPC and tick the route table used by the subnet you will test from. Leave the policy at Full access and create the endpoint. Copy its ID (vpce-...).
    3. Open the bucket, Permissions → Bucket policy → Edit, and paste: 
      {
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{
    "Sid": "DenyReadsOutsideTheEndpoint",
    "Effect": "Deny",
    "Principal": "*",
    "Action": ["s3:GetObject", "s3:PutObject"],
    "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::lab32-vpce-111122223333/*",
    "Condition": { "StringNotEquals": { "aws:SourceVpce": "vpce-REPLACE_ME" } }
  }]
}
      Replace the endpoint ID and bucket name, and choose Save changes.
    4. Open CloudShell from the console toolbar and run aws s3 cp s3://lab32-vpce-111122223333/doc.txt -. The result is AccessDenied, even though your role has full S3 permissions, because an explicit Deny wins.
    5. In CloudShell choose Actions → Create VPC environment. Pick your VPC, the subnet whose route table you selected, and a security group that allows outbound HTTPS. Run the same command in the new tab. It prints the file, because the request now travels through the gateway endpoint. If CloudShell VPC environments are not available to you, use an EC2 instance in that subnet and connect with Session Manager.
    6. Optional, with cost: create an interface endpoint for com.amazonaws.<region>.bedrock-runtime in the same subnet with Enable DNS name on, and replace its policy with the endpoint policy from the network isolation section. From the VPC environment, run nslookup bedrock-runtime.<region>.amazonaws.com and see a private 10.x or 172.x address. Delete the endpoint within the hour.
    CLI and Boto3 path
    1. Create the bucket and the gateway endpoint: 
      export VPCE_BUCKET=lab32-vpce-$ACCOUNT_ID
aws s3 mb s3://$VPCE_BUCKET
echo "internal doc" > doc.txt && aws s3 cp doc.txt s3://$VPCE_BUCKET/doc.txt

VPC_ID=$(aws ec2 describe-vpcs --filters Name=isDefault,Values=true --query 'Vpcs[0].VpcId' --output text)
RTB_ID=$(aws ec2 describe-route-tables --filters Name=vpc-id,Values=$VPC_ID Name=association.main,Values=true \
  --query 'RouteTables[0].RouteTableId' --output text)
VPCE_ID=$(aws ec2 create-vpc-endpoint --vpc-id $VPC_ID --vpc-endpoint-type Gateway \
  --service-name com.amazonaws.$AWS_REGION.s3 --route-table-ids $RTB_ID \
  --query 'VpcEndpoint.VpcEndpointId' --output text)
echo $VPCE_ID
    2. Attach the bucket policy: 
      cat > bucket-policy.json <<EOF
{"Version":"2012-10-17","Statement":[{"Sid":"DenyReadsOutsideTheEndpoint","Effect":"Deny","Principal":"*",
 "Action":["s3:GetObject","s3:PutObject"],"Resource":"arn:aws:s3:::$VPCE_BUCKET/*",
 "Condition":{"StringNotEquals":{"aws:SourceVpce":"$VPCE_ID"}}}]}
EOF
aws s3api put-bucket-policy --bucket $VPCE_BUCKET --policy file://bucket-policy.json

# From your laptop or regular CloudShell: expect AccessDenied
aws s3 cp s3://$VPCE_BUCKET/doc.txt -
    3. From a shell inside the VPC (CloudShell VPC environment or an EC2 instance in a subnet that uses $RTB_ID), the same command succeeds. The equivalent Boto3 check: 
      import boto3
from botocore.exceptions import ClientError

s3 = boto3.client("s3")
try:
    body = s3.get_object(Bucket="lab32-vpce-111122223333", Key="doc.txt")["Body"].read()
    print("allowed:", body.decode())
except ClientError as err:
    print("denied:", err.response["Error"]["Code"])

    Cleanup. Console: delete the bucket policy first (Permissions → Bucket policy → Delete), then empty and delete the bucket; delete the endpoint under VPC → Endpoints → Actions → Delete VPC endpoints (and the optional interface endpoint); delete the CloudShell VPC environment from Actions → Delete. CLI: aws s3api delete-bucket-policy --bucket $VPCE_BUCKET, aws s3 rb s3://$VPCE_BUCKET --force, aws ec2 delete-vpc-endpoints --vpc-endpoint-ids $VPCE_ID.

    Chapter recap

    • A Bedrock workload in private subnets needs one interface endpoint per API it calls (bedrock-runtime, bedrock-agent-runtime, comprehend, kms, and so on) and free gateway endpoints for S3 and DynamoDB. Interface endpoints are billed per hour per Availability Zone.
    • Endpoint policies restrict what passes through an endpoint. A Deny on aws:SourceVpce or aws:SourceVpc in an identity or bucket policy makes the private path mandatory. Exempt the knowledge base service role, because Bedrock reads your bucket from its own network.
    • Keep the app runtime, knowledge base, ingestion, and auditor roles separate, name Bedrock resources by ARN, and protect service role trust policies with aws:SourceAccount and aws:SourceArn. There is no bedrock:Converse action.
    • Lake Formation adds table, column, row, and tag-based permissions on top of the Glue Data Catalog. Use it for structured data lakes; use IAM and bucket policies for document stores.
    • Bedrock prompts and completions are not used for training and are not shared with model providers, who cannot access the model deployment accounts. Customer managed keys can encrypt knowledge base ingestion data, agents, guardrails, prompts, flows, and custom models. Check the newer data retention modes in the current docs.
    • Model invocation logs can hold unmasked PII even when a guardrail masked the response. Encrypt, restrict, expire, and scan them.
    • Macie finds PII at rest in S3 and publishes findings to EventBridge. Comprehend finds and redacts PII in text, in real time or in batch. Guardrail sensitive information filters mask or block PII at the model boundary. Use them as layers.
    • Redaction and type substitution are irreversible. Pseudonymization with a KMS-keyed token and an encrypted vault keeps the most model utility and lets authorized steps restore values. Pseudonymized data is still personal data.
    • S3 Lifecycle rules expire and transition data automatically, including noncurrent versions. Set retention on every log group. Object Lock enforces the opposite: data that must not be deleted.

    Knowledge check

    1. A healthcare company runs a RAG application on Lambda in private subnets with no NAT gateway. Calls to bedrock-runtime time out. Security policy forbids any internet egress. What should the team do?

      Answer

      Create an interface VPC endpoint for com.amazonaws.<region>.bedrock-runtime in the Lambda subnets with private DNS enabled, and attach an endpoint policy limiting it to the application role and approved models. A NAT gateway would fix the timeout but sends traffic over the internet path, which the policy forbids. A gateway endpoint exists only for S3 and DynamoDB, not Bedrock. Moving Lambda out of the VPC removes the timeout but also removes the network isolation the company requires.

    2. A team adds a bucket policy that denies s3:GetObject when aws:SourceVpce is not their gateway endpoint. Their application still works, but the Bedrock knowledge base ingestion job now fails with access denied. What is the least-effort fix that keeps the protection?

      Answer

      Exempt the knowledge base service role from the Deny with an aws:PrincipalArn condition. The knowledge base reads S3 from the Bedrock service network with its own role, so its requests never carry your endpoint ID. Adding more s3:GetObject Allow statements does nothing, because an explicit Deny always wins. Creating an interface endpoint for bedrock-agent does not change where the ingestion reads come from. Removing the bucket policy fixes ingestion but drops the control.

    3. An insurer stores claim documents in hundreds of S3 buckets and wants to know which buckets contain personal data before choosing what to index into a knowledge base. It wants an ongoing view with minimal configuration and cost. Which option fits best?

      Answer

      Amazon Macie automated sensitive data discovery, which samples objects across buckets continuously and scores each bucket's sensitivity. A full one-time Macie job over every bucket works but inspects every object and costs far more. Comprehend cannot crawl S3 buckets by itself, and a batch job per bucket would need custom orchestration. A guardrail only sees text at inference time, so it cannot inventory stored documents.

    4. A support assistant must not pass customers' names and card numbers to the model, but the final reply shown to the verified customer must include their name and the last four digits of the card. Which design meets both requirements?

      Answer

      Pseudonymize with Comprehend detect_pii_entities, replace values with deterministic tokens derived with a KMS HMAC key, store the mapping in a KMS-encrypted DynamoDB table with TTL, and detokenize only the reply sent to the verified customer. A guardrail with ANONYMIZE hides the values but cannot restore them. A Comprehend batch redaction job is irreversible and not suited to the request path. Sending values to the model and relying on the "no training" guarantee does not meet the requirement that the model must not receive them.

    5. An analytics assistant generates SQL against an S3 data lake catalogued in AWS Glue. The marketing team's role must see purchase data only for EU customers and must never see the email column, and new tables marked as public should become available automatically. What should the team use?

      Answer

      AWS Lake Formation: a data filter with a row expression for EU countries that excludes the email column, plus LF-Tag based grants so tables tagged as public are covered as they are added. S3 bucket policies and IAM s3:prefix conditions work at the object level and cannot hide a column or row inside a Parquet file. Maintaining separate filtered copies of the tables works but adds pipelines, storage, and drift. Guardrail filters act on model text, not on what a query engine returns.

    6. An auditor finds full card numbers in Bedrock model invocation logs, even though the application's guardrail anonymizes card numbers in responses. The company must keep invocation logs for 90 days for troubleshooting. Which combination of actions addresses the finding? (Choose the best set.)

      Answer

      Encrypt the log group and S3 log destination with a customer managed KMS key, restrict reads to an auditor role, set 90-day retention on the log group and a 90-day S3 Lifecycle expiration on the log bucket, scan the S3 logs with Macie, and pseudonymize or redact PII in prompts before the model call so it never reaches the log. The guardrail's masking applies to the API response, so the original input can still be logged. Disabling invocation logging fixes the leak but breaks the 90-day troubleshooting requirement. Turning on guardrail trace makes it worse, because trace output echoes the matched sensitive text.

    Project workshop 2 · Build after Chapter 2

    Project 2: A private, PII-safe knowledge assistant

    Chapter 2 gave you the controls one at a time: VPC endpoints, IAM and endpoint policies, KMS keys, PII detection, masking, and retention rules. This workshop puts them around one small RAG assistant for HR documents full of employee data. The model never sees a real name, no request leaves the AWS network, every key and log is yours to control, and data expires when the policy says it should.

    Exam skills practiced

    • 3.2.1 an isolated network with VPC endpoints, endpoint and bucket policies, least-privilege IAM, optional Lake Formation, and CloudWatch and CloudTrail monitoring of data access
    • 3.2.2 Macie discovery of PII in S3, Comprehend PII detection, Bedrock native privacy features, guardrail output filters, and S3 Lifecycle retention
    • 3.2.3 pseudonymization with a token vault, masking, and anonymization that keeps the assistant useful

    Problem statement

    Scenario. A 4,000-person hospital group wants an internal assistant that answers HR policy and case questions: "What is the parental leave policy for night-shift nurses?" or "Summarise the open accommodation requests for the radiology team." The source is about 20,000 documents in S3: policy PDFs, exported HR case notes, and scanned forms. The case notes contain names, employee IDs, phone numbers, home addresses, and occasional health details. The security team has signed off on Amazon Bedrock on one condition: the design must survive the next privacy audit.

    Requirements.

    • The application runs in private subnets with no internet gateway and no NAT gateway. Every AWS call goes through a VPC endpoint.
    • The S3 buckets accept requests only from the project's VPC endpoint and from named service roles. Everything else is denied, even for administrators of the account.
    • All data at rest (source bucket, cleaned bucket, knowledge base, token vault, logs) is encrypted with one customer managed KMS key that the security team can disable.
    • Amazon Macie scans the raw bucket and reports which objects hold PII before anything is indexed.
    • Names, employee IDs, phone numbers, emails, and addresses are replaced with stable pseudonyms before ingestion. Only an approved re-identification role can turn a pseudonym back into a person.
    • Responses pass through a Bedrock guardrail that masks or blocks any PII that survives the earlier steps.
    • Raw uploads expire 30 days after they are cleaned. Model invocation logs are kept for 90 days, then deleted. (Example values. The organisation's retention policy sets the real ones.)
    • Every read of the raw bucket, the token vault, and the knowledge base is recorded and alarmed if the caller is unexpected.

    How the exam phrases it. Expect a stem such as "A healthcare company must ensure that traffic between its application and Amazon Bedrock does not traverse the public internet, and that PII in source documents is not exposed to the foundation model. Which combination of steps meets these requirements with the LEAST operational overhead?" The right answer pairs an interface VPC endpoint (AWS PrivateLink) with a PII step such as Comprehend redaction or a guardrail sensitive information filter. Distractors include a NAT gateway (it still uses the internet), a VPN to Bedrock (not how you reach a regional AWS API), client-side encryption of prompts (the model cannot read ciphertext), and "Bedrock trains on your data, so opt out" (it does not).

    Solution architecture

    The design has two paths that share one VPC, one KMS key, and one set of logs. The ingestion path turns raw HR documents into pseudonymized text the knowledge base can index. The query path answers a question without ever sending a real identifier to the model, then re-identifies the answer only if the caller is allowed to see names.

    flowchart LR
  subgraph VPC["VPC: private subnets only, no IGW, no NAT"]
    RL["Redaction Lambda"]
    QL["Query Lambda"]
    EPB["Interface endpoint: bedrock-runtime"]
    EPA["Interface endpoint: bedrock-agent-runtime"]
    EPC["Interface endpoint: comprehend"]
    EPK["Interface endpoint: kms"]
    GWS["Gateway endpoint: S3"]
    GWD["Gateway endpoint: DynamoDB"]
  end
  RAW["S3 raw bucket (SSE-KMS)"]
  CLEAN["S3 clean bucket (SSE-KMS)"]
  MAC["Amazon Macie job"]
  VAULT["DynamoDB token vault (CMK)"]
  KB["Bedrock knowledge base (CMK)"]
  BR["Bedrock model + guardrail"]
  LOGS["Invocation logs: CloudWatch Logs + S3 (CMK, retention)"]
  TRAIL["CloudTrail data events + alarms"]
  RAW -->|"scan"| MAC
  RAW -->|"S3 event"| RL
  RL --> EPC
  RL --> EPK
  RL --> GWD
  RL --> GWS
  GWS --> CLEAN
  GWD --> VAULT
  CLEAN -->|"ingestion job"| KB
  QL --> EPA
  QL --> EPC
  QL --> EPK
  QL --> GWD
  EPA --> KB
  QL --> EPB
  EPB --> BR
  BR --> LOGS
  RAW -.-> TRAIL
  VAULT -.-> TRAIL
  KB -.-> TRAIL

    Ingestion, step by step. An HR export lands in the raw bucket. A scheduled Macie classification job scans the bucket and writes findings to Security Hub and EventBridge, which gives the security team an inventory of where PII lives and proves the redaction step has work to do. In parallel, an S3 event invokes the redaction Lambda. It runs in the private subnets, reads the object through the S3 gateway endpoint, and calls Comprehend DetectPiiEntities through the Comprehend interface endpoint. For each entity it computes a deterministic pseudonym, for example PERSON_7f3a91, from an HMAC of the normalized value and a secret key. It writes the pseudonym and the original value to the DynamoDB token vault (through the free DynamoDB gateway endpoint), and writes the cleaned text to the clean bucket. A knowledge base ingestion job then indexes only the clean bucket. The raw bucket keeps the original for 30 days for reprocessing, then a Lifecycle rule deletes it.

    Why deterministic pseudonyms. Random tokens would make every mention of the same nurse look like a different person, and the assistant could no longer answer "how many requests has this employee filed?". A keyed HMAC gives the same pseudonym every time for the same input, so relationships in the data survive. The key lives in a KMS HMAC key (the Lambda calls GenerateMac, so the key never leaves KMS) or in Secrets Manager, so nobody can rebuild the mapping by hashing a list of staff names. Either choice needs one more interface endpoint, kms or secretsmanager. This is the trade-off Chapter 2 calls utility versus privacy: masking ([NAME]) is safer but destroys relationships, pseudonymization keeps them but needs a vault you must protect.

    sequenceDiagram
  participant U as HR user
  participant Q as Query Lambda (VPC)
  participant C as Comprehend (endpoint)
  participant V as Token vault
  participant K as Knowledge base (endpoint)
  participant M as Model + guardrail (endpoint)
  U->>Q: Leave balance for Priya Sharma?
  Q->>C: DetectPiiEntities on the question
  C-->>Q: NAME at offset 22
  Q->>Q: HMAC gives PERSON_7f3a91
  Q->>K: Retrieve with pseudonymized question
  K-->>Q: chunks that mention PERSON_7f3a91
  Q->>M: Converse with guardrailConfig
  M-->>Q: answer about PERSON_7f3a91, residual PII masked
  alt caller has re-identification permission
    Q->>V: GetItem PERSON_7f3a91
    V-->>Q: Priya Sharma
  end
  Q-->>U: final answer

    Query, step by step. The question gets the same treatment as the documents. Because pseudonyms are deterministic, "Priya Sharma" in the question becomes the same PERSON_7f3a91 that appears in the indexed chunks, so retrieval still matches. The query Lambda calls Retrieve through the bedrock-agent-runtime endpoint, then Converse through the bedrock-runtime endpoint with a guardrail attached. The guardrail's sensitive information filter is the last line of defense: it uses ANONYMIZE for types such as email and phone, and BLOCK for types that must never appear, such as a US Social Security number. A custom regex catches the hospital's employee ID format. Only if the caller's IAM role is allowed to read the vault does the Lambda swap pseudonyms back to names before returning.

    Key decisions and trade-offs.

    • Redact before indexing and also guard the output. A guardrail alone is too late: the model has already seen the PII, and model invocation logging records the unmasked input and output in plain text, because guardrail masking only changes the API response. Comprehend before ingestion keeps PII out of the index, the prompt, and the logs. The guardrail catches what Comprehend missed.
    • Which endpoints you actually need. Endpoints are needed only for calls your code makes from inside the VPC. Lambda ships its logs through the Lambda service, so a Lambda-only design needs no logs endpoint. S3 calls KMS on your behalf to decrypt SSE-KMS objects, so the Lambda does not need a KMS endpoint for that either. The default build needs four interface endpoints (bedrock-runtime, bedrock-agent-runtime, comprehend, and kms for the HMAC call) and two free gateway endpoints (S3, DynamoDB). A container on ECS Fargate adds ecr.api, ecr.dkr, and logs, and more if it reads secrets.
    • The bucket policy trap. A blanket "deny unless aws:SourceVpce equals my endpoint" also denies the knowledge base's service role. Bedrock reads the clean bucket from the AWS service network, not through your VPC. Exempt the knowledge base role and the Macie service-linked role by aws:PrincipalArn, then test that ingestion still works. You will hit this in milestone 3.
    • Endpoint policies as a second fence. The bedrock-runtime endpoint policy allows bedrock:InvokeModel and bedrock:ApplyGuardrail only on the approved inference profile, model, and guardrail ARNs. A compromised Lambda in this VPC cannot reach any other model, even if its IAM role is too broad. The Converse API is authorized by the bedrock:InvokeModel action, and there is no bedrock:Converse action to allow.
    • One customer managed key, many grants. One CMK keeps the demo simple and lets the security team disable everything at once. Production often uses one key per data class, so that disabling the log key does not take down the knowledge base.

    Technology and services

    RetentionS3 Lifecycle rules (raw: expire after 30 days, expire noncurrent versions after 7 days; invocation log bucket: expire after 90 days) and a CloudWatch Logs retention setting of 30 daysRetention is enforced by the platform, not a cron job you might forget.     MonitoringBedrock model invocation logging to a CMK-encrypted log group and bucket. CloudTrail data events for the raw bucket, the vault table, and AWS::Bedrock::KnowledgeBase. CloudWatch metric filters and alarms. Optional VPC Flow LogsManagement events are logged by default. Data-plane reads (GetObject, GetItem, Retrieve) are not, and those are the reads an auditor asks aboutThree kinds of logs, Monitoring data access
    Compute and hostingDefault: AWS Lambda, zip package, attached to the private subnets. Options:
    • Lambda with a container image, or Lambda Web Adapter running a FastAPI app. Same network model. Use it if dependencies outgrow a zip or you want one FastAPI codebase. Needs ECR endpoints if pulling images privately at deploy time is required by policy.
    • Amazon ECS on AWS Fargate running a FastAPI container. Good for steady traffic and long requests. Adds ecr.api, ecr.dkr, and logs endpoints, and a load balancer.
    • ECS on EC2. Same as Fargate with instances you patch. Choose it only for reserved capacity or GPUs, which this project does not need.
    • Amazon EKS. Powerful but heavy for two functions. Only if the organisation already standardizes on Kubernetes.
    • Amazon EC2 with SSM Session Manager. Useful as a test host inside the VPC. Needs the ssm, ssmmessages, and ec2messages endpoints because there is no internet path.
    • AWS App Runner is dropped: it is closed to new customers, and AWS recommends Amazon ECS Express Mode instead.
    		Lambda needs no servers to patch, costs nothing idle, and shows the VPC egress model with the fewest endpointsDomain 2, hosting options
    Infrastructure as code (infra/)Default: AWS CDK in Python. Options: Terraform (one tool across clouds, explicit state), AWS SAM (shortest for Lambda plus events, weaker for VPC-heavy stacks), CloudFormation (no extra tooling, verbose for a VPC with endpoints), or console and CLI scripts for a single throwaway runA VPC with endpoints, policies, and a key is many resources with cross-references. CDK lets you write them in the language you already useDomain 2, CI/CD for GenAI
    InterfaceDefault: a plain script that calls lambda.invoke from your laptop. The Lambda API is reachable from anywhere, while the function itself runs inside the VPC. Options: an API Gateway private REST API reached through an execute-api interface endpoint (the production pattern for internal users), or a FastAPI app on Fargate behind an internal Application Load BalancerThe script keeps the build focused on data controls. The private API is what you would shipDomain 2, API Gateway

    Build path

    Suggested folder: private-pii-rag/, with src/, tests/, infra/, and README.md, as AGENTS.md describes. If you built the Domain 1 document knowledge platform, copy the chunking and retrieval modules you need from doc-knowledge-platform/ into this folder. Do not import across project folders.

    1. Encrypted buckets and a key. Create the customer managed KMS key with a key policy that names your admin role, the future function roles, and the Macie service-linked role (Macie needs permission to decrypt SSE-KMS objects before it can inspect them; check the Macie documentation on analyzing encrypted objects for the exact statement). Create the raw and clean buckets with SSE-KMS, S3 Bucket Keys, versioning, and Block Public Access. Upload ten synthetic HR documents. Generate them with fake names and IDs. Never use real employee data in a lab.
    2. A VPC with no way out. Create the VPC with two private subnets and no internet gateway or NAT gateway. Add the S3 and DynamoDB gateway endpoints and the four interface endpoints (bedrock-runtime, bedrock-agent-runtime, comprehend, kms) with private DNS. Deploy a hello Lambda in the subnets that calls bedrock-runtime.converse with maxTokens set. It works with no internet route, which shows PrivateLink is carrying the call.
    3. Lock the buckets to the endpoint. Add bucket policies that deny any request where aws:SourceVpce is not your S3 gateway endpoint, except for the knowledge base role and Macie's service-linked role (exempted by aws:PrincipalArn). Try aws s3 cp from your laptop and confirm AccessDenied. Keep a break-glass role exemption so you cannot lock yourself out.
    4. Discover PII with Macie. Enable Macie, add a custom data identifier for the employee ID regex (for example EMP-\d{6}), and run a one-time classification job on the raw bucket. Record which objects have findings and which identifier types appear. This list becomes your test set for redaction.
    5. Pseudonymize on upload. Write the redaction Lambda: DetectPiiEntities on each document (split into pieces under the Comprehend size limit), HMAC pseudonyms, vault writes with a conditional put, and the cleaned object written to the clean bucket. Run it on the ten documents and check the clean bucket has no Macie-detectable PII by running a second Macie job on it.
    6. Index and answer. Create the knowledge base over the clean bucket with the CMK, run an ingestion job, and write the query Lambda: pseudonymize the question, Retrieve, then Converse with the guardrail. Return pseudonyms by default. Add a re-identification path for callers whose role can read the vault.
    7. Logs, retention, and alarms. Turn on model invocation logging to a CMK-encrypted log group (30-day retention) and bucket (90-day Lifecycle expiry). Add the raw bucket's 30-day Lifecycle rule. Enable CloudTrail data events for the raw bucket, the vault table, and the knowledge base. Create a metric filter and alarm that fires when any principal other than the redaction and re-identification roles reads the vault.
    8. Measure privacy and utility. Build a set of 30 questions with expected answers. Run it through three variants: no redaction, masking with [NAME], and HMAC pseudonyms. Record answer accuracy (an LLM judge or manual scoring), how many real identifiers appear in the invocation logs (search them with a regex), p50 and p95 latency added by Comprehend, and Comprehend cost per 1,000 queries. This table is your evidence for the utility versus privacy trade-off.
    9. Prove the failure modes. Run four negative tests and save the outputs: (a) from the query Lambda, urllib.request.urlopen("https://example.com", timeout=5) times out because there is no egress route; (b) calling a model that is not in the endpoint policy returns AccessDeniedException; (c) disabling the KMS key makes retrieval and the vault fail closed (key state changes can take a few minutes to take effect), then re-enabling it restores service; (d) a document with PII that Comprehend misses (an unusual name format or ID) is caught by the guardrail regex, or the test shows the gap and you add an identifier.
    10. Optional: structured HR data with Lake Formation. Register an HR records table in the Glue Data Catalog, grant the assistant role column-level SELECT that excludes salary and diagnosis, and add an Athena query tool. Show that the model cannot retrieve an excluded column even when asked directly.

    Definition of done

    • Running aws ec2 describe-route-tables for the project VPC shows no route to an internet gateway or NAT gateway, and the query Lambda still returns an answer.
    • The egress test in milestone 9 times out, and the test result is saved in tests/.
    • aws s3 cp s3://<raw-bucket>/any.txt . from outside the VPC with your normal role returns AccessDenied. A knowledge base ingestion job on the clean bucket still completes.
    • A Macie job on the clean bucket reports zero findings for the managed and custom identifiers that the raw bucket job reported.
    • A regex search for the synthetic names and employee IDs in the model invocation log group returns no matches after a 30-question test run.
    • Asking about a named person returns an answer about the right pseudonym. With the re-identification role, the same call returns the real (synthetic) name. Without it, it does not.
    • A Converse call through the endpoint to a non-approved model ID fails with AccessDeniedException.
    • aws s3api get-bucket-lifecycle-configuration and aws logs describe-log-groups show the 30-day and 90-day rules and the 30-day log retention.
    • Every bucket, the vault table, the knowledge base, and the log group report the project CMK as their encryption key.
    • A GetItem on the vault by an unexpected role appears as a CloudTrail data event within a few minutes and triggers the alarm.
    • The measurement table from milestone 8 is in the project README.md with a one-paragraph recommendation.

    Cost, Region, and account notes

    Watch out

    • Interface endpoints bill by the hour, per Availability Zone, even when idle, plus a per-GB data processing charge. Four endpoints in two AZs means eight endpoint-hours every hour. In many Regions that runs to tens of dollars a month. Check the AWS PrivateLink pricing page for your Region. Gateway endpoints for S3 and DynamoDB are free. Delete interface endpoints when you stop for the day, or use one AZ for the lab.
    • NAT gateways are not part of this design. If you add one to debug something, it bills per hour plus per GB processed, and it breaks the "no internet" requirement. Remove it.
    • Macie charges for the number of buckets it monitors and for the bytes it inspects in discovery jobs. The 30-day free trial for new accounts covers bucket inventory and automated discovery, not the discovery jobs you create. Scope jobs to one bucket and a small object set. Turn off automated discovery or suspend Macie when you finish. Check current Macie pricing.
    • KMS: each customer managed key costs a monthly fee, and every encrypt and decrypt call is a billed request. Without S3 Bucket Keys, a busy bucket makes a KMS call per object operation. Scheduling a key for deletion has a 7 to 30 day waiting period. Deleting a key makes everything it encrypted unrecoverable, so delete data first.
    • Vector store: Amazon OpenSearch Serverless has a minimum OCU (OpenSearch Compute Unit) charge that bills every hour the collection exists, which can exceed the rest of this project combined. Prefer a Managed Knowledge Base or S3 Vectors for the lab, and delete an OpenSearch Serverless collection the same day you create it. Aurora Serverless v2 bills for its minimum capacity while the cluster runs; recent versions can scale to 0 ACUs and auto-pause when idle if you set the minimum capacity to 0, at the price of a resume delay on the first query. Check that your engine version supports it.
    • Comprehend PII detection is billed per unit of text with a minimum charge per request. Very short questions still pay the minimum. Language support for PII detection is limited, so check the list if your documents are not in English.
    • Invocation logs hold unmasked text. Guardrail masking changes the response only. Anything that reached the model is in the log in plain text. That is why the log group is encrypted, restricted, and short-lived, and why redaction happens before the model call.
    • Region: Managed Knowledge Bases, S3 Vectors, specific models, and Comprehend PII are not in every Region. Check each one in your assigned Region before you build. If one is missing, use a customer-managed KB with S3 Vectors or Aurora, and keep all resources in one Region so the endpoints apply.
    • Account limits: no account restrictions are documented for this repo. If your account limits Regions or services, swap in the fallbacks above.

    Expected spend for a lab run of a few days, with endpoints deleted overnight, a Managed KB or S3 Vectors, a single small Macie job, and about 100 test queries on a small model, should stay in the low tens of dollars. Most of that is interface endpoint hours. The bill grows quickly if you leave endpoints in several AZs running for a month, use OpenSearch Serverless, or enable Macie automated discovery across the whole account. Set an AWS Budgets alert before milestone 2.

    Stretch ideas

    • Add a service control policy in AWS Organizations that denies bedrock:InvokeModel unless aws:SourceVpce matches, so no team can bypass the endpoint. This leads into Chapter 3's organizational governance.
    • Record source attribution on every chunk (document ID, Macie scan ID, redaction version) as knowledge base metadata, and return it with citations. This is the start of Project 3's lineage work and source attribution.
    • Send Macie findings and vault alarms to an EventBridge rule that quarantines the object automatically, a first automated remediation workflow.
    • Add AWS Config rules that check the buckets for SSE-KMS and the log group for retention, and run them on a schedule as continuous compliance evidence.
    • Run the 30-question set through a Bedrock evaluation job with a responsible AI metric, previewing Chapter 4's evaluations.

    Chapter 3 · Task 3.3

    Implement AI Governance and Compliance Mechanisms

    Chapters 1 and 2 stopped bad content and kept data private. Governance answers a different question: six months from now, can you prove what the system did, why it did it, which data it used, and who approved it? This chapter builds the evidence trail that an auditor, a regulator, or your own incident review will ask for. You will document models with SageMaker AI model cards, register data sources in the AWS Glue Data Catalog, carry source attribution from S3 all the way into a citation, keep audit and decision logs, set guardrails and model rules across a whole AWS organization, and watch for misuse and drift with alarms that fix things on their own.

    Exam skills covered

    • 3.3.1 compliance frameworks: programmatic model cards in SageMaker AI, automatic data lineage with AWS Glue, metadata tagging for source attribution, decision logs in CloudWatch Logs
    • 3.3.2 data source tracking: registering sources in the Glue Data Catalog, source attribution metadata in FM-generated content, CloudTrail audit logging
    • 3.3.3 organizational governance: frameworks that align FM use with organizational policy, regulation, and responsible AI principles
    • 3.3.4 continuous monitoring and advanced controls: automated detection of misuse, drift, and policy violations, bias drift monitoring, alerting and remediation, token-level redaction, response logging, AI output policy filters

    How this task is examined

    Task 3.3 questions usually describe an organization that has a GenAI application working and now faces an external demand: a regulator, an internal audit, a customer security questionnaire, or an incident that nobody can reconstruct. The question asks which combination of services produces the missing evidence or control with the least effort. Read each scenario with four questions.

    1. Is the ask about documentation, traceability, or enforcement? Documentation points to model cards. Traceability points to lineage, metadata, and logs. Enforcement points to IAM, service control policies, and organization-level guardrails.
    2. Which layer must the evidence come from? Who called which API is CloudTrail. What the model was asked and what it answered is model invocation logging. Why the application made a decision is your own decision log.
    3. Is the scope one application, one account, or the whole organization? A requirement that says "every account" or "every team" rules out answers that depend on each developer remembering to do something.
    4. Is it a one-time check or a continuous one? "Detect", "alert", and "automatically remediate" mean metrics, alarms, EventBridge, and Lambda. A report assembled once a quarter is not monitoring.

    What you need first

    This chapter assumes you know the three kinds of logs from the Orientation: CloudTrail for API activity, CloudWatch Logs for application output, and Bedrock model invocation logging for prompts and completions. If those are blurry, read the logging section of the Orientation first. You also need IAM policy basics from Domain 1, IAM for a Bedrock developer, and the guardrail anatomy from Chapter 1.

    Compliance frameworks for FM deployments (3.3.1)

    A compliance framework is a published list of requirements (called controls) that an organization commits to meeting, and a way to show that it meets them. For a backend developer, the useful mental model is a test suite for the organization: each control is an assertion, and the evidence is the test output. Your job as the engineer is to make the system produce that evidence automatically, so that an audit becomes a query and not a two-week archaeology project.

    What AI regulation and standards tend to ask for

    The exam does not test the text of any specific law. It tests whether you can map a generic requirement to an AWS mechanism. The frameworks you will hear about most often are the EU AI Act (a European regulation that sorts AI systems into risk tiers and puts the heaviest documentation, logging, and human-oversight duties on high-risk systems), ISO/IEC 42001 (an international standard for an AI management system, in the same family as ISO 27001 for information security), and the NIST AI Risk Management Framework (a voluntary US framework organized around the functions Govern, Map, Measure, and Manage). Their details and timelines change, and legal interpretation is for your compliance team. What they share is a small set of recurring demands.

    Recurring demandWhat it means in practiceAWS mechanism that produces evidenceSection
    Document the model and its limitsIntended use, out-of-scope use, known risks, evaluation results, ownerSageMaker AI model cards; AWS AI Service Cards for Amazon models; provider model cards for third-party modelsModel cards
    Know where the data came fromA register of data sources, owners, and the transformations between themAWS Glue Data Catalog, crawler lineage, Amazon DataZone or SageMaker Catalog lineageLineage
    Trace an output back to its sourcesEach answer can show which documents and versions it usedS3 object metadata, knowledge base metadata files, citations from RetrieveAndGenerateAttribution
    Keep records of activityWho did what, when; what the model was asked and answered; why the app decidedCloudTrail, model invocation logging, structured decision logs in CloudWatch LogsAudit
    Apply policy consistentlyRules hold for every team, not only careful onesService control policies, Bedrock policies in AWS Organizations, IAM condition keys, AWS ConfigOrg governance
    Monitor after deploymentDetect misuse, drift, and violations, and respondCloudWatch metrics and alarms, anomaly detection, EventBridge, Lambda, SageMaker Model Monitor with ClarifyMonitoring

    Exam tip

    When a scenario names a regulation, ignore the name and look for the verb: "document" is model cards, "trace" is lineage and attribution, "record" or "audit" is CloudTrail and logs, "enforce across accounts" is AWS Organizations policies, "detect" is CloudWatch alarms. A distractor often offers a service that sounds compliance-flavoured but does a different job, for example Amazon Macie (finds sensitive data in S3) when the question asks who invoked a model (CloudTrail).

    AWS Artifact and the shared responsibility split

    AWS Artifact is a self-service portal in the console where you download AWS's own compliance reports (for example SOC reports and ISO certificates) and accept agreements such as a Business Associate Addendum for health data. It is evidence about AWS's half of the shared responsibility model: that the Bedrock service itself is operated under audited controls. It says nothing about your application. If an auditor asks "is Amazon Bedrock in scope for ISO 27001", Artifact and the AWS Services in Scope pages answer. If they ask "does your chatbot log every decision", only your own architecture answers.

    Turning controls into evidence

    The pattern that scales is to write each control as a statement that a machine can check, then wire a source of evidence to it. The diagram shows the flow for a typical GenAI workload.

    flowchart LR
  P["Organizational policy and regulation"] --> C["Control list (for example: every model call uses an approved guardrail)"]
  C --> E1["Preventive: SCP, Bedrock policy, IAM condition"]
  C --> E2["Detective: CloudTrail, invocation logs, AWS Config rule"]
  C --> E3["Documentary: model card, data catalog entry"]
  E1 --> V["Evidence store (S3 with Object Lock, log groups)"]
  E2 --> V
  E3 --> V
  V --> R["Audit query or report"]

    Every control should have at least one preventive or detective mechanism. A control that only exists in a policy document is a promise. For example, "all model invocations use the corporate guardrail" becomes a Bedrock policy in AWS Organizations (preventive), a CloudTrail data event selector on guardrails plus a CloudWatch metric (detective), and a section in each model card that names the guardrail version (documentary). Evidence is only useful if nobody can quietly edit it, so put long-term evidence in an S3 bucket with S3 Object Lock (a setting that makes objects write-once for a retention period) or enable CloudTrail log file validation, which writes signed digest files so you can prove that log files were not changed after delivery.

    Backend analogy

    Preventive controls are input validation and database constraints. Detective controls are monitoring and the error tracker. Documentary controls are the README and the architecture decision records. A codebase with only a README and no constraints passes review and still corrupts data. The same holds for a GenAI system with only a responsible AI policy PDF.

    Programmatic model cards with SageMaker AI (3.3.1)

    A model card is a structured document that describes a model: what it is for, what it must not be used for, how it was evaluated, what risks are known, and who owns it. Amazon SageMaker AI Model Cards is the AWS feature that stores model cards as versioned resources in your account, with a fixed JSON schema, an approval status, and export to PDF. The exam phrase is "programmatic model cards": create and update them from code in your pipeline, so the card changes when the model, the prompt, or the evaluation results change.

    Two things surprise newcomers. First, a SageMaker model card does not require a SageMaker-hosted model. You can write a card for an application that calls a Bedrock foundation model and leave the training sections empty, which is the normal case for this exam. Second, AWS and the model providers publish their own documentation for the base model. AWS publishes AI Service Cards for its first-party models and AI services (for example Amazon Nova and Amazon Titan models), and third-party providers publish model cards for theirs. Your card documents your use of the model: the system prompt, the guardrail, the knowledge base, the evaluation you ran, and the limits you found. Link to the provider's card instead of copying it.

    The card's structure

    The card's Content is a JSON string that follows the model card schema. These are the sections you use for a Bedrock application. Field names below come from the schema; check the current schema in the SageMaker AI documentation before relying on an optional field.

    Section keyUseful fieldsWhat to put there for a Bedrock app
    model_overviewmodel_name, model_description, model_version, model_creator, model_owner, problem_typeThe application name, the Bedrock model ID or inference profile, the provider, and your owning team
    intended_usespurpose_of_model, intended_uses, factors_affecting_model_efficiency, risk_rating, explanations_for_risk_ratingSupported and unsupported uses; risk_rating is one of High, Medium, Low, Unknown
    business_detailsbusiness_problem, business_stakeholders, line_of_businessWhy the system exists and who signed off
    evaluation_detailsA list of entries with name, evaluation_observation, evaluation_job_arn, metric_groupsResults of Bedrock evaluation jobs from Chapter 4, including fairness and toxicity metrics
    additional_informationethical_considerations, caveats_and_recommendations, custom_detailsKnown failure modes, the guardrail ID and version, the prompt version, data sources, and links to the provider's card

    A card moves through four statuses: Draft, PendingReview, Approved, and Archived. Each update creates a new card version, so the history of what was approved and when is kept for you. A common governance rule is "nothing reaches production unless its card is Approved", checked by a step in the deployment pipeline.

    Creating, approving, and exporting a card from code

    The script below creates a card for a Bedrock-backed claims assistant, moves it to review, and exports a PDF to S3. The sagemaker Boto3 client owns model cards, even though the model is in Bedrock.

    import json
import time

import boto3

sm = boto3.client("sagemaker")

CARD_NAME = "claims-assistant"
content = {
    "model_overview": {
        "model_name": "claims-assistant",
        "model_description": "Answers adjuster questions about policy wording using a Bedrock knowledge base.",
        "model_version": 3,
        "model_creator": "Anthropic (base model), Claims Platform team (application)",
        "model_owner": "claims-platform@example.com",
        "problem_type": "Retrieval-augmented question answering",
    },
    "intended_uses": {
        "purpose_of_model": "Help licensed adjusters find and quote policy clauses.",
        "intended_uses": "Internal adjusters only. Answers must cite a policy document.",
        "factors_affecting_model_efficiency": "Accuracy drops on scanned PDFs with poor OCR and on policies older than 2015.",
        "risk_rating": "Medium",
        "explanations_for_risk_rating": "Output informs, but does not decide, claim outcomes. A human approves every payout.",
    },
    "business_details": {
        "business_problem": "Reduce time adjusters spend searching policy documents.",
        "business_stakeholders": "Head of Claims; AI Governance Board",
        "line_of_business": "Property insurance",
    },
    "additional_information": {
        "ethical_considerations": "Must not infer protected attributes. Guardrail blocks denied topic 'claim-approval-decision'.",
        "caveats_and_recommendations": "Not for customer-facing use. Not for legal advice.",
        "custom_details": {
            "bedrock_model_id": "us.anthropic.claude-sonnet-4-5-20250929-v1:0",
            "guardrail": "arn:aws:bedrock:us-east-1:111122223333:guardrail/gr4abc123xyz:4",
            "prompt_version": "arn:aws:bedrock:us-east-1:111122223333:prompt/PRMT12345:7",
            "knowledge_base_id": "KB12345678",
        },
    },
}

sm.create_model_card(
    ModelCardName=CARD_NAME,
    Content=json.dumps(content),
    ModelCardStatus="Draft",
    Tags=[{"Key": "owner", "Value": "claims-platform"}],
)

# Ready for the governance board. Each update creates a new card version.
sm.update_model_card(ModelCardName=CARD_NAME, ModelCardStatus="PendingReview")

job_arn = sm.create_model_card_export_job(
    ModelCardName=CARD_NAME,
    ModelCardExportJobName=f"{CARD_NAME}-{int(time.time())}",
    OutputConfig={"S3OutputPath": "s3://governance-evidence-111122223333/model-cards/"},
)["ModelCardExportJobArn"]

while True:
    job = sm.describe_model_card_export_job(ModelCardExportJobArn=job_arn)
    if job["Status"] in ("Completed", "Failed"):
        print(job["Status"], job.get("ExportArtifacts", {}).get("S3ExportArtifacts"))
        break
    time.sleep(5)

    The model ID, guardrail, and prompt ARNs above are example values; use the ones from your own account and Region. Treat the card as code: generate content from the same configuration file your application reads, and run the script in the deployment pipeline. If the guardrail version changes and the card does not, your documentation is already wrong.

    Watch out

    A model card records what you wrote. It does not check that the application actually uses the guardrail version the card names. Pair every claim in a card with a detective control: a CloudTrail or invocation log query that confirms the guardrail ARN on real traffic, or an organization-level enforced guardrail that makes the claim true by construction.

    Data lineage with the AWS Glue Data Catalog (3.3.1, 3.3.2)

    Data lineage is the record of where a piece of data came from and what was done to it on the way. For a RAG application the chain is: a source system, a raw S3 prefix, a cleaning or chunking job, a processed S3 prefix, the knowledge base data source, the vector index, and finally an answer. When an answer turns out to be wrong, lineage tells you which upstream document and which job version produced the chunk the model quoted.

    The Glue Data Catalog as the data source register

    The AWS Glue Data Catalog is a managed metadata store: a list of databases (logical groups) and tables (each one describing a dataset's location, format, and schema), shared by Glue, Athena, Lake Formation, and other analytics services. It does not hold your data. It holds the description of it. A Glue crawler is a managed job that reads a location such as an S3 prefix, infers the format and schema, and creates or updates catalog tables. For governance you use the catalog as the register of every data source an FM application is allowed to read, with table parameters and tags for owner, classification, and retention.

    flowchart LR
  SRC["Policy admin system"] -->|"nightly export"| RAW[("s3://claims-raw/policies/")]
  RAW -->|"Glue job: clean and chunk"| PROC[("s3://claims-processed/policies/")]
  PROC -->|"data source sync"| KB["Bedrock knowledge base KB12345678"]
  KB --> ANS["Answer with citations"]
  CR["Glue crawler"] -.->|"registers"| CAT["Glue Data Catalog: db claims_kb_sources"]
  RAW -.-> CR
  PROC -.-> CR
  CAT -.->|"lineage graph"| DZ["Amazon DataZone or SageMaker Catalog"]

    Read the diagram from left to right for the data path and along the dotted lines for metadata. The crawler registers both the raw and processed prefixes as tables, so the catalog knows they exist, their schema, and their owner tags. The Glue job between them is where transformations happen; when you run transformations as Glue jobs, the job definition itself becomes part of the lineage record.

    Where lineage graphs come from

    The catalog alone gives you an inventory. A lineage graph (A fed B fed C) needs a service that captures the relationships. Glue crawlers have a LineageConfiguration setting with CrawlerLineageSettings set to ENABLE or DISABLE, and Amazon DataZone (now also surfaced in Amazon SageMaker Unified Studio as the SageMaker Catalog) can capture and display lineage from sources such as Glue crawlers and Glue jobs, using the OpenLineage format. Which sources are captured automatically and how the console presents the graph have changed several times, so check the current DataZone and SageMaker Catalog documentation before you depend on a specific behaviour. For the exam, the association to remember is: register sources with the Glue Data Catalog, track lineage automatically with Glue.

    Exam tip

    "Register data sources" and "automatically track data lineage" point to AWS Glue (Data Catalog and crawlers). "Grant column-level or row-level access to catalog tables" points to AWS Lake Formation, which Chapter 2 covers. "Find PII inside the objects" points to Amazon Macie. The catalog describes data; it does not scan its contents for sensitive values.

    A knowledge base does not read from the Glue Data Catalog. It reads from its data source (for example an S3 prefix). The catalog sits beside that path as the register, which is why you must keep them aligned: the knowledge base data source should point only at prefixes that have a catalog table, and a scheduled check can compare the two lists.

    Metadata tagging and source attribution (3.3.2)

    Source attribution means every generated answer can say which sources it used, down to the document and its version. The exam pairs it with "metadata tagging": you attach descriptive key-value pairs to data at the point of storage, and you carry them through retrieval into the answer. There are three different kinds of tags in play, and mixing them up is a common mistake.

    KindAttached toWho reads itUse it for
    AWS resource tagsResources: buckets, knowledge bases, guardrails, inference profiles, model cardsIAM (attribute-based access control), Cost Explorer, AWS Config, Resource GroupsOwnership, cost allocation, environment, data classification of the whole resource
    S3 object tagsOne S3 object (up to 10 tags)S3 Lifecycle rules, IAM conditions on s3:ExistingObjectTag, your codePer-object classification and retention, for example classification=confidential
    Knowledge base metadataEach chunk, from a sidecar file named <document>.metadata.jsonBedrock Knowledge Bases: metadata filters at query time, returned with every retrieval resultAttribution in answers (owner, version, effective date) and filtering

    Only the third kind reaches the model's answer. S3 object tags are not copied into the vector index. If you want an attribute to appear in a citation, it must be in the sidecar metadata file next to the document.

    Writing metadata that survives retrieval

    For a document s3://claims-processed/policies/HO-2024-03.pdf, place a file HO-2024-03.pdf.metadata.json in the same prefix. Bedrock reads it during ingestion and attaches the attributes to every chunk from that document. There is a size limit on the file and a limit on attributes per chunk; check the knowledge base quotas page for the current values.

    {
  "metadataAttributes": {
    "source_system": "policy-admin",
    "document_id": "HO-2024-03",
    "document_version": "7",
    "effective_date": "2024-03-01",
    "owner": "policy-wording-team",
    "catalog_table": "claims_kb_sources.policies_processed"
  }
}

    The catalog_table attribute is the join between source attribution and lineage: from any citation you can jump to the Glue Data Catalog table, and from there to the job that produced it. The pipeline that writes the processed document should write its sidecar in the same step, so the two can never disagree.

    Carrying attribution into the response

    RetrieveAndGenerate on the bedrock-agent-runtime client returns citations: for each span of generated text, the retrieved references that support it, each with its location (for example the S3 URI) and the chunk's metadata. Retrieval results also include built-in metadata keys such as x-amz-bedrock-kb-source-uri. The function below turns citations into an attribution block the UI can show and the decision log can store.

    import boto3

kb_rt = boto3.client("bedrock-agent-runtime")


def answer_with_sources(question: str, kb_id: str, model_arn: str) -> dict:
    resp = kb_rt.retrieve_and_generate(
        input={"text": question},
        retrieveAndGenerateConfiguration={
            "type": "KNOWLEDGE_BASE",
            "knowledgeBaseConfiguration": {"knowledgeBaseId": kb_id, "modelArn": model_arn},
        },
    )
    sources = []
    for citation in resp.get("citations", []):
        for ref in citation.get("retrievedReferences", []):
            meta = ref.get("metadata", {})
            sources.append({
                "uri": ref.get("location", {}).get("s3Location", {}).get("uri"),
                "document_id": meta.get("document_id"),
                "document_version": meta.get("document_version"),
                "owner": meta.get("owner"),
                "catalog_table": meta.get("catalog_table"),
            })
    # De-duplicate: several spans often cite the same chunk.
    unique = {(s["uri"], s["document_version"]): s for s in sources}
    return {"answer": resp["output"]["text"], "sources": list(unique.values()), "session_id": resp.get("sessionId")}

    If you build your own pipeline with Retrieve and Converse instead, you own attribution: keep the retrieved results, ask the model to cite chunk numbers in a structured output (see Chapter 1, structured outputs), and map the numbers back to metadata in code. Never let the model write the source URI itself, because it will invent plausible ones.

    Exam tip

    "Each response must show which document version it came from" points to knowledge base metadata plus citations. "Track spend per team on Bedrock" points to resource tags on application inference profiles and cost allocation tags. "Delete objects tagged as temporary after 30 days" points to S3 object tags with a Lifecycle rule. Same word "tag", three different answers.

    Audit logging and decision logs (3.3.1, 3.3.2)

    An audit asks three questions, and each has a different log as its answer. The Orientation introduced the three logs; here is how they line up against audit questions, and the detail the exam tests about CloudTrail event types.

    Audit questionLogContainsDoes not contain
    Who called which API, from where, and was it allowed?AWS CloudTrailCaller identity, time, source IP, API name, request parameters such as modelId, error codeThe prompt and completion text
    What exactly was the model asked, and what did it answer?Bedrock model invocation logging (to CloudWatch Logs, S3, or both)Request and response bodies, token counts, model ID, caller identityYour business context: which customer, which case, why
    Why did the application do what it did?Your decision log in CloudWatch LogsRequest ID, user, prompt version, guardrail outcome, sources, confidence, action takenAnything you forget to write

    CloudTrail: management events and data events for Bedrock

    CloudTrail records two categories that matter here. Management events are recorded by default, kept for 90 days in Event history at no charge, and delivered to a trail if you create one. Data events are high-volume operations that CloudTrail records only when you add an event selector to a trail, and they cost extra. Bedrock splits its APIs between the two in a way that the exam can test.

    OperationCloudTrail categoryTo capture it
    InvokeModel, InvokeModelWithResponseStream, Converse, ConverseStreamManagementRecorded by default
    Control plane calls: CreateGuardrail, UpdateGuardrail, DeleteGuardrail, CreateKnowledgeBase, PutModelInvocationLoggingConfigurationManagementRecorded by default
    Retrieve, RetrieveAndGenerateDataEvent selector for resource type AWS::Bedrock::KnowledgeBase
    InvokeAgent / InvokeInlineAgentDataAWS::Bedrock::AgentAlias / AWS::Bedrock::InlineAgent
    InvokeFlowDataAWS::Bedrock::FlowAlias
    ApplyGuardrail, including guardrail evaluations during model invocationDataAWS::Bedrock::Guardrail
    StartAsyncInvoke, GetAsyncInvoke, InvokeModelWithBidirectionalStreamDataAWS::Bedrock::Model and async invoke resource types

    Two details from the Bedrock CloudTrail documentation are worth knowing. Streaming calls produce an extra event after the stream ends, with eventType set to AwsServiceEvent, carrying the token counts; it is not an API call and was not authorized by IAM, so filter on eventType = 'AwsApiCall' when you count invocations or check whether a policy blocked something. And Amazon GuardDuty, the threat detection service, analyzes CloudTrail and can flag suspicious Bedrock activity such as an unusual identity removing guardrails.

    Watch out

    Event history covers 90 days, one account, one Region. An auditor who asks for a year of evidence needs a trail that delivers to S3 (with log file validation and ideally Object Lock), and an organization trail if there are several accounts. AWS CloudTrail Lake, the SQL query store for events, stopped accepting new customers on 31 May 2026; existing users keep it. For a new account, deliver the trail to S3 and query it with Amazon Athena, or deliver it to CloudWatch Logs and use Logs Insights.

    Decision logs: the log only you can write

    A decision log is a structured record your application writes for every request that matters: the inputs it considered, the controls that ran, and the outcome. CloudWatch Logs stores it, and CloudWatch Logs Insights (a query language over log groups) reads it. Write one JSON object per line, so that Insights discovers fields automatically. Put correlation IDs in every record so you can join it to the invocation log and CloudTrail.

    sequenceDiagram
  participant U as Adjuster
  participant A as App (Lambda)
  participant G as Guardrail
  participant K as Knowledge base
  participant L as CloudWatch Logs
  U->>A: question, user_id
  A->>G: ApplyGuardrail (INPUT)
  G-->>A: NONE
  A->>K: RetrieveAndGenerate
  K-->>A: answer and citations
  A->>G: ApplyGuardrail (OUTPUT)
  G-->>A: GUARDRAIL_INTERVENED (PII masked)
  A->>L: decision record with request_id, sources, guardrail actions
  A-->>U: masked answer and sources
    import json
import logging
import time
import uuid

logger = logging.getLogger()
logger.setLevel(logging.INFO)


def log_decision(*, user_id: str, prompt_version: str, guardrail: str, input_action: str,
                 output_action: str, sources: list[dict], model_id: str, latency_ms: int,
                 outcome: str, bedrock_request_id: str | None) -> str:
    """Write one structured decision record. Never log raw prompts here; the invocation log holds them."""
    decision_id = str(uuid.uuid4())
    logger.info(json.dumps({
        "type": "decision",
        "decision_id": decision_id,
        "ts": int(time.time() * 1000),
        "user_id": user_id,
        "model_id": model_id,
        "prompt_version": prompt_version,
        "guardrail": guardrail,
        "guardrail_input_action": input_action,
        "guardrail_output_action": output_action,
        "source_ids": [f'{s["document_id"]}@{s["document_version"]}' for s in sources],
        "latency_ms": latency_ms,
        "outcome": outcome,                  # for example answered, refused, escalated
        "bedrock_request_id": bedrock_request_id,
    }))
    return decision_id

    With that shape, the questions an auditor asks become Logs Insights queries. The first finds every answer in a period that relied on a specific document version, which is what you need when that document turns out to be wrong. The second counts guardrail interventions by user, the starting point for misuse detection in the monitoring section.

    fields @timestamp, decision_id, user_id, outcome
| filter type = "decision" and source_ids like /HO-2024-03@7/
| sort @timestamp desc

fields user_id
| filter type = "decision" and (guardrail_input_action = "GUARDRAIL_INTERVENED" or guardrail_output_action = "GUARDRAIL_INTERVENED")
| stats count(*) as interventions by user_id
| sort interventions desc
| limit 20

    Backend analogy

    CloudTrail is the API gateway access log. Model invocation logging is request and response body capture. The decision log is your domain event stream. You would never debug a payment bug from the access log alone, and you cannot answer "why did the assistant say that" from CloudTrail alone.

    Organizational governance systems (3.3.3)

    Skill 3.3.3 is phrased abstractly ("comprehensive frameworks that align with organizational policies, regulatory requirements, and responsible AI principles"), and the exam turns it into concrete scenarios: forty teams in twenty accounts are adopting Bedrock, and the CISO wants the same rules everywhere without reviewing every pull request. Governance at that scale has a people part and a platform part. The people part decides the rules. The platform part makes it impossible, or at least visible, to break them.

    The people part: who decides what

    Most organizations set up an AI governance board or a responsible AI council, with a written policy that classifies use cases by risk and states what each class requires. A minimal split of responsibilities looks like this. The exam will not ask for a RACI chart, but scenario answers often hinge on "who approves" and "who is accountable".

    DecisionGovernance boardPlatform teamApplication teamSecurity and compliance
    Which models are approved for which data classesDecidesEnforces in policyRequestsAdvises
    Baseline guardrail (content filters, PII handling)ApprovesBuilds and enforces org-wideAdds use-case guardrailsReviews
    Model card for a use caseApproves high-risk cardsProvides the pipelineWrites and maintainsReviews
    Logging, retention, and evidenceSets requirementsProvides org trail and log archiveWrites decision logsAudits
    Incident response for harmful outputInformedRuns automated remediationFixes root causeLeads

    The platform part: guardrails for the organization

    AWS Organizations groups many AWS accounts under one management account, arranged in organizational units (OUs) such as Sandbox, Dev, and Prod. It offers several policy types. Two of them matter most here.

    A service control policy (SCP) sets the maximum permissions for every IAM principal in the accounts it is attached to. It never grants anything; it only removes. An SCP that denies a Bedrock action wins over any IAM policy in the member account. SCPs do not apply to the management account itself, which is one reason to run no workloads there. The SCP below allows only an approved list of model families in the Prod OU. Because a cross-Region inference profile routes requests to the foundation model in other Regions, the resource patterns use a wildcard Region and cover both the inference profile and the foundation model ARNs.

    {
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Sid": "DenyUnapprovedModels",
      "Effect": "Deny",
      "Action": [
        "bedrock:InvokeModel",
        "bedrock:InvokeModelWithResponseStream"
      ],
      "NotResource": [
        "arn:aws:bedrock:*::foundation-model/anthropic.claude-sonnet-4-5*",
        "arn:aws:bedrock:*::foundation-model/amazon.nova-*",
        "arn:aws:bedrock:*::foundation-model/amazon.titan-embed-text-v2*",
        "arn:aws:bedrock:*:*:inference-profile/*",
        "arn:aws:bedrock:*:*:application-inference-profile/*"
      ]
    }
  ]
}

    Leaving every inference profile ARN out of the deny is safe in this pattern because a call through an inference profile is also authorized against the underlying foundation model ARNs, and those are still checked by the deny. Test the policy with an unapproved model through a profile before you rely on it. Converse and ConverseStream are authorized by the bedrock:InvokeModel and bedrock:InvokeModelWithResponseStream actions; there is no bedrock:Converse IAM action, so a policy that names one does nothing. The model families in the example are illustrations; build your list from the model IDs approved by your board and check them in the Bedrock console for your Region.

    Amazon Bedrock policies are a newer AWS Organizations policy type (BEDROCK_POLICY) that enforces a guardrail on every model invocation in the target accounts, with no change to application code. You create a guardrail and a numeric version in the management account, share it with the organization through a resource-based policy that grants bedrock:ApplyGuardrail, enable the policy type, and attach a policy that names the guardrail ARN and version per Region. A single account can do the same for itself with account-level enforcement (PutEnforcedGuardrailConfiguration on the bedrock client, once per Region). When organization, account, and request-level guardrails all apply, Bedrock enforces all of them, and the most restrictive setting wins. Enforced guardrails support content filters, denied topics, word filters, sensitive information filters, and contextual grounding checks; the documentation warns against including an automated reasoning policy in an enforced guardrail.

    MechanismScopeWhat it guaranteesGap
    Guardrail passed in each request (guardrailConfig)One callThat call is filteredDepends on every developer remembering
    IAM condition key bedrock:GuardrailIdentifierOne role or userCalls without the named guardrail are deniedDocumented limitations with RetrieveAndGenerate and agents, which make internal model calls; input tagging can narrow what is checked on input
    Account-level enforced guardrailOne account and RegionEvery invocation in the account is filteredEach account must be configured
    Bedrock policy in AWS OrganizationsOUs or the whole organizationEvery invocation in target accounts is filtered, centrally managedNeeds Organizations; configure per Region
    SCP denying unapproved modelsOUs or the whole organizationOnly approved models can be calledSays nothing about content

    Exam tip

    "Ensure every team's Bedrock calls use the corporate guardrail, with the least operational overhead and no code changes" points to guardrail enforcement through Amazon Bedrock policies in AWS Organizations. "Prevent any account in the Prod OU from using models that are not approved" points to an SCP. A distractor offers a shared Lambda wrapper library, which works only for teams that adopt it.

    Detective controls: AWS Config, Control Tower, and Audit Manager

    AWS Config records the configuration of supported resources over time and evaluates them against Config rules (AWS managed rules or your own). Custom rules are a Lambda function or a Guard policy that returns COMPLIANT or NON_COMPLIANT. Some governance facts are account settings, not resources; for those a periodic custom rule works well. For example, a rule that runs daily, calls bedrock.get_model_invocation_logging_configuration(), and reports NON_COMPLIANT when invocation logging is off. Config coverage of Bedrock resource types varies by Region and changes over time, so check the list of supported resource types before you assume a managed rule exists. A conformance pack deploys a set of rules to many accounts at once.

    AWS Control Tower sets up a governed multi-account environment (a landing zone) with a log archive account, an audit account, an organization CloudTrail trail, and a catalog of controls: preventive ones implemented as SCPs, detective ones implemented as Config rules, and proactive ones that check CloudFormation resources before they are created.
